norms. We will show that by selecting slightly more than
d
vectors, we can in fact get a subset of vectors such that the coefficient vector satisfies the much stronger guarantee of being bounded in
\ell_{2}
. We also show that without this relaxation of choosing more than
d
vectors, such a result is not possible. Our result are based on the theory of coresets for John ellipsoids.

We give the following definition:

Definition 3.3

(
\ell_{p}
-Well-Conditioned Spanning Set)
.

Let
p>0
. Let
\{\mathbf{a}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\varepsilon\geq 0
. Then,
\{\mathbf{a}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
for a subset
S\subseteq[n]
is a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate
\ell_{p}
-well-conditioned coreset
if for each
i\in[n]
, there exists
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
such that
\mathbf{a}_{i}=\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{\top}\mathbf{x}
and

\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq 1+\varepsilon.

(8)

3.2.1
Coresets via Coordinate Ascent

Our first result uses results on coresets for Löwner–John ellipsoids
[
Tod16
]
, and gives a deterministic algorithm based on coordinate ascent which selects
O(d\log\log d)
unweighted rows of
\mathbf{A}
that is sufficient to approximate a John ellipsoid for all the rows.

Theorem 3.4

(Coresets for Löwner–John Ellipsoids, Proposition 3.17,
[
Tod16
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
\varepsilon>0
. There exists
S\subseteq[n]
with
\left\lvert S\right\rvert=O(d\log\log d+d/\varepsilon)
and nonnegative weights
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
supported on
S
such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{u}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
,
\left\lVert\mathbf{u}\right\rVert_{1}=d
, and

(1+\varepsilon)\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathbf{x}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{x}\leq 1\right\}\supseteq\conv(\left\{\pm\mathbf{a}_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{n})

Furthermore,
S
and
\mathbf{u}
can be computed in
\tilde{O}((d/\varepsilon)(\nnz(\mathbf{A})+d^{2}))
time.

That is, there exists a set
S
of
O(d\log\log d)
rows of
\mathbf{A}
and an
O(1)
-approximate John ellipsoid for
\mathbf{A}|_{S}
containing all rows of
\mathbf{A}
. This gives the following corollary:

Corollary 3.5

(
\ell_{2}
-Well-Conditioned Spanning Set via Coordinate Ascent)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Let
S\subseteq[n]
be the subset given by the algorithm in in Theorem
3.4
. Then,
\{\mathbf{a}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
is a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate
\ell_{2}
-well-conditioned spanning set.

Proof.

We take the coefficients to be
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{\top})^{-}\mathbf{a}_{i}
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{\top})^{-}\mathbf{a}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{\top}\mathbf{A}|_{S})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{u}|_{S})\mathbf{A}|_{S})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{u}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1

\displaystyle=\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\varepsilon

Theorem
3.4

as claimed.
∎

We also note here that Theorem
3.4
also yields unweighted coresets for
\ell_{\infty}
subspace embeddings.

Corollary 3.6

(
\ell_{\infty}
Subspace Embedding)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. There exists
S\subseteq[n]
with
\left\lvert S\right\rvert=O(d\log\log d)
such that for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(\sqrt{d})\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

Furthermore,
S
can be computed in
\tilde{O}(d\nnz(\mathbf{A})+d^{3})
time.

Proof.

Let
S\subseteq[n]
and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be given by Theorem
3.4
with
\varepsilon=1/2
. By scaling, it suffices to prove that

\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1\right\}\subseteq\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1\right\}\subseteq O(\sqrt{d})\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1\right\}

The first inclusion is immediate, so it suffices to prove the second inclusion.

Note that by taking polars in the inclusion in the result of Theorem
3.4
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\leq 1\right\}

\displaystyle=((1+\varepsilon)\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathbf{x}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{x}\leq 1\right\})^{\circ}

\displaystyle\subseteq(\conv(\left\{\pm\mathbf{a}_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}))^{\circ}=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1\right\}

Now suppose that
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
. Then,
\left\lvert\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq 1
for every
i\in[n]
, so we have that

\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{u}_{i}\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{u}_{i}\leq d

and thus

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left\lVert\mathbf{A}_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\leq d\right\}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{d}\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\leq 1\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{d}\left\{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1\right\}

which was the desired result.
∎

We note that if only
d
rows are selected, as opposed to
O(d)
, then a result like Theorem
3.5
is not possible:

Theorem 3.7
.

There exists a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
for
n=d+1
such that for any subset
S
of
d
rows of
\mathbf{A}
, we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{-\top}\mathbf{a}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}=\sqrt{d}

for
[n]\setminus S=\{i\}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be the identity matrix concatenated with the all ones vector. If the all ones vector is not selected as a part of
S
, then we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{-\top}\mathbf{a}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}=\sqrt{d}

On the other hand, if one of the standard basis vectors
\mathbf{e}_{i}
for
i\in[d]
is not selected as a part of
S
, then
\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{-\top}\mathbf{a}_{i}
is
-1
on the entry corresponding to a standard basis vector, and
1
on the entry corresponding to the all ones vector. Thus, we again have

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{-\top}\mathbf{a}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}=\sqrt{d}.

∎

3.2.2
Spanning Sets via Leverage Score Sampling

Our second result uses leverage score sampling to obtain a significantly faster algorithm, at the expense of randomization and a slightly larger coreset. For this result, we use a much faster John ellipsoid algorithm due to
[
CCLY19
]
which does not a priori yield coresets. We show how to use sampling to turn this result into a coreset.

Theorem 3.8

(Theorem 3.6,
[
CCLY19
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
\varepsilon>0
. There is an algorithm which computes nonnegative weights
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{u}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
,
\left\lVert\mathbf{u}\right\rVert_{1}=d
, and

\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}\leq 1+\varepsilon

Furthermore, the algorithm runs in
\tilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-2}(\nnz(\mathbf{A})+d^{\omega}))
time.

We now give our result for obtaining coresets via sampling:

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be weights computed in Theorem
3.8
. Let
\beta=O(\varepsilon^{-2}(\log d)(\log\frac{1}{\delta}))
and suppose we sample independently sample each
i\in[n]
with probability
p_{i}=\min\{1,(1+\varepsilon)\beta\mathbf{u}_{i}\}
to form
S\subseteq[n]
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,
\{\mathbf{a}_{i}\}_{i\in S}
is a
(1+O(\varepsilon))
-approximate
\ell_{2}
-well-conditioned coreset, and

\left\lvert S\right\rvert\leq O(1)\varepsilon^{-2}d(\log d)\log\frac{1}{\delta}.

Furthermore, with probability at least
1-\delta
, simultaneously for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{1+\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\sqrt{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty},

that is,
S
is a coreset for an
\ell_{\infty}
subspace embedding.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbf{u}
satisfies

\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(\diag(\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\mathbf{A})=\mathbf{u}_{i}^{1/2}\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{u}_{i}^{1/2}\mathbf{a}_{i}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{u}_{i}.

(9)

Thus,
(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{u}_{i}
are leverage score upper bounds for
\diag(\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}
. Thus, if we sample rows
S\subseteq[n]
as in the theorem statement and scale each sampled row by
\mathbf{s}_{i}=1/\sqrt{p_{i}}
, then we have that

\left\lVert\diag(\mathbf{s})\diag(\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}=(1\pm\varepsilon)\left\lVert\diag(\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}

(10)

for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see, e.g.,
[
CLM+15
, Lemma 4]
). Also note that for each
i\in[n]
,
\mathbf{s}_{i}^{2}\mathbf{u}_{i}\leq 1
for every
i\in[n]
. Thus,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{-\top}\mathbf{a}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{\top}\mathbf{A}|_{S})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{s})^{2}\diag(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A}|_{S})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\diag(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{a}_{i}

(
10
)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)^{2}

Theorem
3.8

so
S
is a
(1+O(\varepsilon))
-approximate
\ell_{2}
-well-conditioned coreset.

To see that
\mathbf{A}|_{S}
is an
\ell_{\infty}
subspace embedding, first note that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}}\left\lVert\diag(\mathbf{s})\diag(\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\varepsilon)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}}\left\lVert\diag(\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}

(
10
)

Now note that for any
i\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{i}\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(\diag(\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\mathbf{A})\left\lVert\diag(\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

properties of leverage scores

\displaystyle\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathbf{u}_{i}\left\lVert\diag(\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

(
9
)

so
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\left\lVert\diag(\mathbf{u})^{1/2}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}
. Furthermore, for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

Combining these bounds yields that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{1+\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\sqrt{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}

for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
∎

3.3
Applications: Subspace Embeddings with Large Distortion

We now obtain several new results on coresets for subspace embeddings using our new notion of well-conditioned coresets.

3.3.1
Average Top
k
Subspace Embeddings

We start with a generalization of the
\ell_{\infty}
loss known as the
average top
k
loss
. The various benefits of considering this loss function is studied in depth by
[
FLYH17
]
.

Definition 3.10

(Average Top
k
Loss
[
FLYH17
]
)
.

Let
k\in[n]
. For
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the
average top
k
loss
is defined as

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{[i]}\right\rvert,

where
\mathbf{y}_{[i]}
denotes the
i
th largest entry in
\mathbf{y}
, with ties broken arbitrarily.

We obtain the following subspace embedding results:

Theorem 3.11

(Average Top
k
Subspace Embedding, Small
k
)
.

Let
S\subseteq[n]
be an
O(1)
-approximate
\ell_{2}
-well-conditioned coreset. Let
k\leq\left\lvert S\right\rvert
. Then for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}\leq O(\sqrt{k\left\lvert S\right\rvert})\cdot\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}.

For instance, we can use the result of Corollary
3.5
and set
\left\lvert S\right\rvert=O(d\log\log d)
, so that we obtain an algorithm which samples
O(d\log\log d)
rows and achieves a distortion of
O(\sqrt{kd\log\log d})
.

Proof.

We assume without loss of generality that
\left\lvert S\right\rvert
is a multiple of
k
, by reading more entries if needed.

By the guarantee of
S
, for each
i\in[n]
, we have that
\mathbf{a}_{i}=\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{c}
for some
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{c}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=O(1)
. Then for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
i\in[n]
, we have that

\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{2}=\left\lvert\sum_{j\in S}\mathbf{c}_{j}\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle\right\rvert^{2}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{c}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\sum_{j\in S}\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{2}=O(1)\sum_{j\in S}\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{2}.

Now consider partitioning
S
into
\left\lvert S\right\rvert/k
subsets of size
k
, say
S=\bigcup_{l=1}^{\left\lvert S\right\rvert/k}S_{l}
. Then,

\sum_{j\in S}\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{2}=\sum_{l=1}^{\left\lvert S\right\rvert/k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S_{l}}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\sum_{l=1}^{\left\lvert S\right\rvert/k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S_{l}}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}\leq\frac{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}{k}\cdot k^{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}^{2}

so by combining these two bounds, we have that

\frac{1}{k}\left\lvert\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{x}\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq O(1)\sqrt{\left\lvert S\right\rvert/k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}.

Summing over the
k
indices witnessing
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}
yields

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}\leq O(\sqrt{k\left\lvert S\right\rvert})\cdot\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}.\qed

Theorem 3.12

(Average Top
k
Subspace Embedding, Large
k
)
.

Let
k\geq k_{0}
for some
k_{0}=O(d+\log\frac{1}{\delta})
. Let
N_{1},N_{2},\dots,N_{k/t}
be a random partition of
[n]
into
k/t
parts for some
t=O(d+\log\frac{1}{\delta})
. Let
S_{l}
be
O(1)
-approximate
\ell_{2}
-well-conditioned coresets for
\mathbf{A}|_{N_{l}}
for each
l\in[k/t]
, each of size at most
s
, and let
S=\bigcup_{l=1}^{k/t}S_{l}
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, simultaneously for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}\leq O(\sqrt{ts})\cdot\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}

For instance, we can use the result of Corollary
3.5
and set
s=O(d\log\log d)
, so that we obtain an algorithm which samples
O(k\log\log d)
rows and achieves a distortion of
O(d\sqrt{\log\log d})
with constant probability.

Proof.

We first fix a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}=1
. By Chernoff bounds, with probability at least
1-\delta/\exp(10d)
, each of the partitions
N_{l}
contains at most
O(t)
elements of the top
k
entries of
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
. Then conditioned on this event, by the guarantee of Theorem
3.11
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l=1}^{k/t}O(t)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{N_{l}}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{O(t)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l=1}^{k/t}\sqrt{t\left\lvert S_{l}\right\rvert}\cdot O(t)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S_{l}}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{O(t)}}

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\sqrt{ts}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{O(k)}}

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\sqrt{ts}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}

A standard net argument over the
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathsf{AT}_{k}}
-unit ball in the column space of
\mathbf{A}
then completes the argument.
∎

3.3.2
Cascaded Norm Subspace Embeddings

Next, we show several results for embedding a subspace of
matrices
under various cascaded norms, which are matrix norms formed by taking norms of rows using one norm, and then taking another norm of the vector formed by the row norms
[
JW09
]
.

Our first result is an embedding for the cascaded
(\infty,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert)
-norm, which takes an arbitrary norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
and outputs the maximum value over the
n
rows.

Theorem 3.13

(
(\infty,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert)
-Subspace Embedding)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
be any norm on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Define the
cascaded
(\infty,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert)
-norm
of an
n\times m
matrix
\mathbf{B}
as

\left\lVert\mathbf{B}\right\rVert_{\infty,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}\coloneqq\max_{i=1}^{n}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}\rVert

Let
S\subseteq[n]
be an
O(1)
-
\ell_{2}
-well-conditioned coreset. Then, for every
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{\infty,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{\infty,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}\leq O(\sqrt{\lvert S\rvert})\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{\infty,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}.

Proof.

For any
i\in[n]
, we write
\mathbf{a}_{i}=\mathbf{A}|_{S}^{\top}\mathbf{x}
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=O(1)
, as given by the definition of an
O(1)
-
\ell_{2}
-well-conditioned coreset. Then for any
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\Big\lVert\sum_{j\in S}\mathbf{x}_{j}\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\Big\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in S}\lvert\mathbf{x}_{j}\rvert\cdot\lVert\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\rVert

triangle inequality and homogeneity

\displaystyle\leq\left\lparen\sum_{j\in S}\lvert\mathbf{x}_{j}\rvert^{2}\right\rparen^{1/2}\left\lparen\sum_{j\in S}\lVert\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\rVert^{2}\right\rparen^{1/2}

Cauchy–Schwarz

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{\lvert S\rvert})\cdot\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|_{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{\infty,\lVert\cdot\rVert}.

Taking the max over
i\in[n]
yields the claim.
∎

This result is perhaps surprising, in that it
directly
sparsifies the structure of the norm. This is in contrast to what we can prove for the cascaded
(p,\lVert\cdot\rVert)
-norm, for which we need to embed the
\lVert\cdot\rVert
norm into the
\ell_{2}
norm first, which causes an
O(d)
factor loss:

Theorem 3.14
.

Let
p\geq 1
. Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
be any norm on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Define the
cascaded
(p,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert)
-norm
of an
n\times m
matrix
\mathbf{B}
as

\left\lVert\mathbf{B}\right\rVert_{\infty,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}\coloneqq\left\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{B}\rVert^{p}\right\rparen^{1/p}

Suppose that
\mathbf{S}
satisfies

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\kappa\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}

for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, for every
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}|\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{p,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{p,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}\leq O(\kappa d)\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{p,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}.

Proof.

We first obtain a result for
m=d
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert=\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}
. In this case, Dvoretzky’s theorem
[
Dvo61
]
(see also
[
SW18
]
) states that for a
d^{O(p)}\times d
Gaussian matrix
\mathbf{G}
, with constant probability, we have for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that

\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}=\Theta(1)\lVert\mathbf{G}\mathbf{x}\rVert_{p}.

Then, by the guarantee of
\mathbf{S}
,

\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{G}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}\leq\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{G}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}\leq\kappa\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{G}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}.

Note that Dvoretzky’s theorem ensures that

\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{G}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}=\Theta(1)\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\rVert_{p,2}

and

\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{G}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}=\Theta(1)\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\rVert_{p,2}

for every
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, so we have that

\Omega(1)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{p,2}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{p,2}\leq O(\kappa)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{p,2}.

Next, we use Löwner–John ellipsoids to show that the previous result in fact implies a result for general cascading
(p,\lVert\cdot\rVert)
-norms as well, and in
m
dimensions. Fix an
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
. We then consider the symmetric convex body given by
K\coloneqq\{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\lVert\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\rVert\leq 1\}
. Then, there exists a
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that the ellipsoid
E\coloneqq\{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\lVert\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\rVert_{2}\leq 1\}
satisfies
E\subseteq K\subseteq\sqrt{d}E
. Note then that by the above guarantee for
\mathbf{S}
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{p,\lVert\cdot\rVert}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{H}\right\rVert_{p,2}

E\subseteq K

\displaystyle\leq O(\kappa)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{H}\right\rVert_{p,2}

\displaystyle\leq O(\kappa\sqrt{d})\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{p,\lVert\cdot\rVert}

K\subseteq\sqrt{d}E

\displaystyle\leq O(\kappa\sqrt{d})\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{H}\right\rVert_{p,2}

E\subseteq K

\displaystyle\leq O(\kappa\sqrt{d})\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{H}\right\rVert_{p,2}

\displaystyle\leq O(\kappa d)\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{p,\lVert\cdot\rVert}

\displaystyle\text{$K\subseteq\sqrt{d}E$}\qed

4
Nearly Optimal Oblivious
\ell_{p}
Subspace Embeddings

We first show the following lemma, which shows how to apply Corollary
3.5
, even when the set of vectors is a whole subspace of points, rather than a finite set.

Lemma 4.1

(Well-Conditioned Spanning Sets for Subspaces of
\ell_{p}
)
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
and let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. There exists
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times s}
for
s=O(d\log\log d)
such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{e}_{i}\right\rVert_{p}=1
, for every
i\in[s]
, and for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}=1
, there exists
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{s}
such that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{y}
and
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{2}\leq O(1)
.

Proof.

Our proof proceeds by handling a net over the
\ell_{p}
ball in the column space of
\mathbf{A}
using Corollary
3.5
, and the difference from the net using Lewis bases.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be to be determined. By a standard volume argument, there exists a set
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}=1
, there exists
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\varepsilon
, with
\lvert\mathcal{N}\rvert\leq(1/\varepsilon)^{O(d)}
. We may then apply Corollary
3.5
to identify a set
\mathcal{S}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{N}
of size at most
s_{1}=\lvert\mathcal{S}_{1}\rvert\leq O(d\log\log d)
such that for any
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
, there exists
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{1}}
such that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}_{1}\mathbf{y}
and
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{2}\leq O(1)
, where
\mathbf{R}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times s_{1}}
is the matrix which enumerates the vectors of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
in its columns. Note that this proves the result for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{N}
.

We now let
\mathbf{R}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a
\ell_{p}
Lewis change of basis matrix for
\mathbf{A}
. We then let
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(s_{1}+d)}
be the horizontal concatenation of
\mathbf{R}_{1}
and
\mathbf{R}_{2}
. Now let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be any vector with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}=1
. Then, we can find
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
that satisfies
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\varepsilon
. By the prior paragraph, we may write
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}_{1}\mathbf{y}_{1}
for some
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{1}}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}=O(1)
. On the other hand, if we write
\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
uniquely as a linear combination
\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{R}_{2}\mathbf{y}_{2}
of the columns of
\mathbf{R}_{2}
, then letting
\mathbf{W}
be the diagonal matrix for
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{y}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}_{2}\mathbf{y}_{2}\rVert_{2}

Definition
2.1

\displaystyle\leq d^{0\lor(1/2-1/p)}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}_{2}\mathbf{y}_{2}\right\rVert_{p}

Lemma
2.5

\displaystyle\leq d^{0\lor(1/2-1/p)}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle\leq d^{0\lor(1/2-1/p)}\varepsilon

Then by taking
\varepsilon=1/\sqrt{d}
, we obtain
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}=O(1)
. Thus, we can write

\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}_{1}\mathbf{y}_{1}+\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}_{2}\mathbf{y}_{2}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{y}

for some
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{1}+d}
with

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{y}_{1}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\mathbf{y}_{2}\right\rVert_{2}=O(1)

as claimed.
∎

Given the above lemma, the proof of Theorem
1.11
is immediate:

Proof of Theorem
1.11
.

We simply translate the guarantees of Lemma
4.1
into that of Theorem
1.11
. First, we take
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}
, where
\mathbf{R}
is given by Lemma
4.1
. Then, the entrywise
\ell_{p}
norm of
\mathbf{U}
is bounded since

\left\lVert\mathbf{U}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}=\sum_{j=1}^{s}\left\lVert\mathbf{U}\mathbf{e}_{j}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}=\sum_{j=1}^{s}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{e}_{j}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}=s.

Next, let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfy
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}=1
. Then, by Lemma
4.1
, we may identify a
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s}
such that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{z}
and

\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{2}\leq O(1)\leq O(1)\cdot\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}=O(1)\left\lVert\mathbf{U}\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}.

The result for general
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
follows by scaling.
∎

A related result we can obtain is a well-conditioned factorization of a matrix. This result sharpens
[
BRW21
, Lemma 9]
, who obtained a similar result using Auerbach bases.

See
1.13

Proof.

We first write
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{L}^{\prime}\mathbf{D}
where
\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the diagonal matrix with
\left\lVert\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\right\rVert_{p}
as its
j
th diagonal entry, end
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
has columns with unit
\ell_{p}
norm. Next, we apply Corollary
3.5
to obtain a set
S\subseteq[d]
with
s=O(k\log\log k)
columns such that for each
j\in[d]
, there exists
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{2}\leq O(1)
such that
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}\mathbf{e}_{j}=\mathbf{L}^{\prime}|^{S}\mathbf{z}
. We may then set
\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{V}^{\top}=\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{D}
, where
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times d}
is the matrix with
\mathbf{z}
in its columns. This clearly satisfies the conditions of the theorem.
∎

4.1
A Proof of Corollary
1.12

We provide a proof of Corollary
1.12
. For simplicity, we present a simple proof based on the
\ell_{1}
embeddings of
[
SW11
]
, which has suboptimal running time. By using techniques shown in
[
MM13
,
WW19
]
, it is possible to use a more sophisticated algorithm running in input sparsity time with similar guarantees, by using our Theorem
1.11
in a similar way.

Proof of Corollary
1.12
.

We take
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times d}
to be drawn with i.i.d.
p
-stable random variables
[
Nol20
]
, scaled by
C/r^{1/p}
for some large enough constant
C
. For every
(i,j)\in[r]\times[d]
,
\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{e}_{j}
is distributed as
C\left\lVert\mathbf{U}\mathbf{e}_{j}\right\rVert_{p}/r^{1/p}
times a
p
-stable variable
X_{i,j}
, by definition of
p
-stable variables. With probability at least
1-1/\poly(rd)
,
\lvert X_{i,j}\rvert
is at most
\poly(rd)
, so by a union bound over all
rd
choices of
(i,j)
, this is true for every
(i,j)\in[r]\times[d]
. Call this event
\mathcal{E}
. Conditioned on this event, the expectation of
\lvert X_{i,j}\rvert
is
O(\log(rd))
, so by linearity of expectation, we have

\E\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}|\mathcal{E}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\E\left[\left\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{e}_{j}\right\rvert^{p}|\mathcal{E}\right]\leq O(1)\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{U}\mathbf{e}_{j}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}}{r}=O(\left\lVert\mathbf{U}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}\log(rd)).

By Markov’s inequality, this bound holds up to constant factors with probability at least
199/200
. We condition on this event. Then, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, we write
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{z}
for
\mathbf{z}
promised by Theorem
1.11
, so that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{z}\right\rvert^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{q}^{p}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lvert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U}\right\rvert_{p}^{p}

Hölder’s inequality

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{U}\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{U}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{U}\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}O(\left\lVert\mathbf{U}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}\log(rd))

\displaystyle\leq O(d\log(rd)\log\log d)\left\lVert\mathbf{U}\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}.

Taking
p
th roots gives the upper inequality.

For the lower inequality, we use
[
WW19
, Lemma 2.12]
:

Lemma 4.2

(Lemma 2.12 of
[
WW19
]
)
.

Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be independent
p
-stable random variables. Then for sufficiently large
n
and
T
,

\Pr\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert X_{i}\rvert^{p}\geq L_{p}n\log\frac{n}{\log T}\right\}\geq 1-\frac{1}{T}

for some constant
L_{p}
.

For every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}=1
,
\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}
is the sum of
r
independent
p
-stable random variables, raised to the
p
and scaled by
r
. We then apply the above lemma with
n=r
and
T=\exp(r)
to conclude that for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}=1
,
\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\geq 1
with probability at least
1-\exp(-r)
, by choosing our constant
C
large enough. By a standard net argument (see, e.g.,
[
SW11
]
), this is true for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}=1
. This in turn implies the lower tail inequality for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by scaling.
∎

5
Sharp Column Subset Selection for
g
-Norm Low Rank Approximation

In this section, we show our new results on column subset selection for the
g
-norm, also known as
M
-estimators.

Algorithm 1

Column Subset Selection for
M
-Estimators

input:
Input matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, rank
k
, loss function
g
.

output:
Subset
T\subseteq[d]
of
O(k\log^{2}d)
columns.

1:

T_{0}\leftarrow[d]

2:

s\leftarrow O(k\log\log k)
\triangleright
Given by Corollary
3.5

3:

while

\lvert T_{l}\rvert\geq 1000s

do

4:

t_{l}\leftarrow 160s\log_{2}d_{l}

5:

for

t=1,2,\dots,O(\log\log d)

do

6:

Sample
H\sim\binom{T_{l}}{t_{l}}

7:

Let
\mathbf{x}^{j}
minimize
\min_{\mathbf{x}}\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{g}
up to a
\mathsf{reg}_{g,t_{l}}
factor for each
j\in T_{l}

8:

Let
F_{l,t}
be the
d_{l}/960=\lvert T_{l}\rvert/960
columns with smallest regression cost
\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}^{j}-\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{g}

9:

C_{l,t}\leftarrow\sum_{j\in F_{l,t}}\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}^{j}-\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{g}

10:

Let
t^{*}
be the
t
with smallest
C_{l,t}

11:

T_{l+1}\leftarrow T_{l}\setminus F_{l,t^{*}}

5.1
An Improved Structural Result on Uniform Sampling

We first give a slight more useful form of Corollary
3.5
to our setting.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\mathbf{A}_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be a rank
k
matrix. Then, there exists a set
S\subseteq[d]
of size
s=O(k\log\log k)
such that for every
j\in[d]
,

\lVert(\mathbf{A}_{*}|^{S})^{-}\mathbf{a}_{*}^{j}\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq O(1).

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{A}_{*}
has rank
k
, we can write
\mathbf{A}_{*}=\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{R}
for some orthonormal
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
. Then by Corollary
3.5
, there exists a set
S\subseteq[d]
of size
s
such that for every
j\in H\cup\{i\}
, we have that
\lVert(\mathbf{R}|^{S})^{-}\mathbf{r}^{j}\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq O(1)
. The result then follows since

\displaystyle\lVert(\mathbf{A}_{*}|^{S})^{-}\mathbf{a}_{*}^{j}\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{a}_{*}^{j})^{\top}(\mathbf{A}_{*}|^{S})^{-\top}(\mathbf{A}_{*}|^{S})^{-}\mathbf{a}_{*}^{j}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{r}^{j})^{\top}\mathbf{Q}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}(\mathbf{R}|^{S})^{-\top}(\mathbf{R}|^{S})^{-}\mathbf{Q}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{r}^{j}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{r}^{j})^{\top}(\mathbf{R}|^{S})^{-\top}(\mathbf{R}|^{S})^{-}\mathbf{r}^{j}

\displaystyle=\lVert(\mathbf{R}|^{S})^{-}\mathbf{r}^{j}\rVert_{2}^{2}.\qed

Using Lemma
5.1
, we now obtain the following lemma, which gives an improved version of Lemmas 2.1 and 2.2 of
[
SWZ19b
]
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
\mathbf{A}_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be any rank
k
matrix and let
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}_{*}
. Let
s\geq O(k\log\log k)
and let
H\sim\binom{[d]}{2s}
and let
i\sim[d]\setminus H
. Let
R=R(H\cup\{i\})
be the set of size
s
given by Lemma
5.1
for
\mathbf{A}_{*}|^{H\cup\{i\}}
. The following hold:

•

With probability at least
1/2
,
i\notin R

•

If
i\notin R
, then there is
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}
such that

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}^{i}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}\leq O(1)\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}^{2}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}^{2}}\sum_{j\in H\cup\{i\}}\left\lVert\mathbf{d}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}

(11)

•

With probability at least
1/4
over
H\sim\binom{[d]}{2s}
,

\left\lvert\left\{i\in[d]\setminus H:i\notin R(H\cup\{i\})\right\}\right\rvert\geq\frac{d}{4}

Proof.

By symmetry,
i
is a uniformly random index of
H\cup\{i\}
, so
\Pr\{i\notin R\}\geq 1-s/(2s+1)>1/2
, which gives the first conclusion.

Let
\alpha_{j}
denote the
j
th entry of
(\mathbf{A}_{*}|^{R})^{-}\mathbf{a}^{i}_{*}
for each
j\in R
and
\alpha_{j}=0
for
j\in H\setminus R
. We then have that

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}}\Big\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}^{i}\Big\rVert_{g}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\lVert\sum_{j\in H}\alpha_{j}\mathbf{a}^{j}-\mathbf{a}^{i}\Big\rVert_{g}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\lVert\sum_{j\in H}\alpha_{j}(\mathbf{a}_{*}^{j}+\mathbf{d}^{j})-(\mathbf{a}_{*}^{i}+\mathbf{d}^{i})\Big\rVert_{g}

\displaystyle=\Big\lVert\sum_{j\in R}\alpha_{j}\mathbf{d}^{j}-\mathbf{d}^{i}\Big\rVert_{g}

since
\mathbf{A}_{*}|^{R}(\mathbf{A}_{*}|^{R})^{-}\mathbf{a}_{*}^{i}=\mathbf{a}_{*}^{i}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}\Big\lparen\sum_{j\in R}\lVert\alpha_{j}\mathbf{d}^{j}\rVert_{g}+\lVert\mathbf{d}^{i}\rVert_{g}\Big\rparen

approximate triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}}\Big\lparen\sum_{j\in R}\alpha_{j}\lVert\mathbf{d}^{j}\rVert_{g}+\lVert\mathbf{d}^{i}\rVert_{g}\Big\rparen

at least linear growth

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}}\Big\lparen\Big\lparen\sum_{j\in R}\alpha_{j}^{2}\Big\rparen^{1/2}\Big\lparen\sum_{j\in R}\lVert\mathbf{d}^{j}\rVert_{g}^{2}\Big\rparen^{1/2}+\lVert\mathbf{d}^{i}\rVert_{g}\Big\rparen

Cauchy–Schwarz

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}}\Big\lparen\Big\lparen\sum_{j\in R}\lVert\mathbf{d}^{j}\rVert_{g}^{2}\Big\rparen^{1/2}+\lVert\mathbf{d}^{i}\rVert_{g}\Big\rparen.

Squaring both sides yields the second conclusion.

The third conclusion follows from the same proof as Lemma 2.2 of
[
SWZ19b
]
.
∎

5.2
Sharper Guarantees for the
[
SWZ19b
]
Algorithm

We now use the result of Lemma
5.2
to improve the analysis of the
[
SWZ19b
]
algorithm.

5.2.1
Level Sets

Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{*}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}
, where
\mathbf{A}_{*}
is the best rank
k
approximation in the
g
-norm. Let the columns of
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
be
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{1},\boldsymbol{\delta}^{2},\dots,\boldsymbol{\delta}^{d}
. To gain fine-grained control over the costs of the columns, we will need to consider a partition of the columns into
O(\log d)
level sets based on
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Then:

•

Let
s=O(k\log\log k)
denote the maximum size of an
\ell_{2}
-well-conditioned subset given by Corollary
3.5
in
k
dimensions.

•

Let
T_{l}\subseteq[d]
denote the subset of columns surviving after the
l
th round of the algorithm. We assume without loss of generality that
T_{l}=[d_{l}]
for some
d_{l}\leq d
. Furthermore, we assume without loss of generality that
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{1}\rVert_{g}\geq\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{2}\rVert_{g}\geq\dots\geq\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{d_{l}}\rVert_{g}
.

•

Let
\mathsf{Res}_{l}\coloneqq\sum_{j=d_{l}/4}^{d_{l}}\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}
denote the residual cost, after restricting to the surviving columns and after removing the columns with cost in the top quarter.

•

Let

R_{l}^{i}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\left\{j\in[d_{l}]\setminus[d_{l}/4]:\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}\leq\frac{1}{d_{l}^{2}}\mathsf{Res}_{l}\right\}&\text{if $i=\infty$}\\
\left\{j\in[d_{l}]\setminus[d_{l}/4]:2^{-i}\cdot\mathsf{Res}_{l}<\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}\leq 2^{-i+1}\cdot\mathsf{Res}_{l}\right\}&\text{if $0<i<2\log_{2}d_{l}$}\end{cases}

Recall that our goal is to show that with constant probability, the
d_{l}/80
columns with the smallest regression cost when fit on
\mathbf{A}|^{H}
each have a cost of at most
O(\sqrt{k\log\log k})\mathsf{Res}_{l}/d_{l}
. We first show that we may assume with out loss of generality that
R_{l}^{\infty}
is small in cardinality.

Lemma 5.4
.

If
\left\lvert R_{l}^{\infty}\right\rvert>d_{l}/4
, then with probability at least
1/6
over the randomness of
H
,

\left\lvert\left\{j\in T_{l}:\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}\leq\frac{1}{d_{l}}\mathsf{Res}_{l}\right\}\right\rvert\geq\frac{1}{80}d_{l}

Proof.

Note that
\E\lvert R_{l}^{\infty}\cap H\rvert\geq 20s
. By Chernoff bounds, with probability at least
99/100
, we have that
\left\lvert R_{l}^{\infty}\cap H\right\rvert\geq 4s\geq 2k
. Then by conditioning on the size of
R_{l}^{\infty}\cap H
, we can apply the same proof from Lemma 2.5 of
[
SWZ19b
]
restricted to
R_{l}^{\infty}
to show that with probability at least
1/5-1/100\geq 1/6
over the randomness of
H
,

\left\lvert\left\{j\in T_{l}:\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}\leq\frac{\lvert H\rvert}{d_{l}^{2}}\mathsf{Res}_{l}\right\}\right\rvert\geq\frac{1}{20}\left\lvert R_{l}^{\infty}\right\rvert\geq\frac{1}{20}\cdot\frac{d_{l}}{4}=\frac{1}{80}d_{l}.

Note that
\lvert H\rvert\leq d_{l}
, which gives the claimed result.
∎

By Lemma
5.4
, we may assume that
\lvert R_{l}^{\infty}\rvert\leq d_{l}/4
. In this case, we show that we must have many columns which belong to a large level set.

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose that
\lvert R_{l}^{\infty}\rvert\leq d_{l}/4
. Then, at least
d_{l}/4
columns belong to a level set
R_{l}^{i}
such that
\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert\geq d_{l}/8\log_{2}d_{l}
.

Proof.

Note that the number of columns which can belong in a level set of size less than
d_{l}/8\log_{2}d_{l}
is less than

2(\log_{2}d_{l})\cdot\frac{d_{l}}{8\log_{2}d_{l}}=\frac{d_{l}}{4}

since there are only
2\log_{2}d_{l}
level sets. Since there are at most
d_{l}/4
columns in
R_{l}^{\infty}
and at most
d_{l}/4
that are excluded for being in the top quarter, we conclude as desired.
∎

5.2.2
Fitting a Constant Fraction of Columns

We will now show that we can fit a constant fraction of columns in a large level set with small cost. We first show the following lemma for a single level set:

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
i\in[2\log_{2}d_{l}]
be such that
\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert\geq d_{l}/8\log_{2}d_{l}
. Then, with probability at least
1/6
, there are at least
\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert/20
indices
j\in R_{l}^{i}
such that there exists
\mathbf{x}
satisfying

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}^{j^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{g}\leq O(\sqrt{s})\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}}\frac{\mathsf{Res}_{l}}{2^{i}}

Proof.

The proof is based on adapting Lemmas 2.3, 2.4, and 2.5 of
[
SWZ19b
]
.

Note that
\mathbf{E}\lvert R_{l}^{i}\cap H\rvert\geq 20s
. By Chernoff bounds, with probability at least
99/100
, we have that
\lvert R_{l}^{\infty}\cap H\rvert\geq 4s
. We condition on this event. Then, let
H^{\prime}\subseteq R_{l}^{i}\cap H
be a uniformly random subset of
R_{l}^{i}\cap H
of size
2s
. Then by Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\Pr_{H^{\prime}}\left\{\sum_{j\in H^{\prime}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}\geq 40\frac{s}{\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert}\sum_{j\in R_{l}^{i}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\E\left[\sum_{j\in H^{\prime}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}\right]}{40\frac{s}{\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert}\sum_{j\in R_{l}^{i}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}}\leq\frac{\frac{2s}{\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert}\sum_{j\in R_{l}^{i}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}}{40\frac{s}{\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert}\sum_{j\in R_{l}^{i}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{20}

Furthermore, by an averaging argument, we have that

\Bigg\lvert\Bigg\{j^{\prime}\in R_{l}^{i}:\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}\geq\frac{5}{\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert}\sum_{j\in R_{l}^{i}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}\Bigg\}\Bigg\rvert\leq\frac{1}{5}\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert

Now note that
H^{\prime}
is a uniformly random subset of
R_{l}^{i}
of size
2s
. Then, by Lemma
5.2
, we have that with probability at least
1/4
, there are at least
\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert/4
indices
j^{\prime}\in R_{l}^{i}
for which (
11
) holds. Thus, for at least
\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert/4-\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert/5=\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert/20
indices
j^{\prime}\in R_{l}^{i}
, we have that

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{H^{\prime}}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H^{\prime}}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}^{j^{\prime}}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}^{2}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}^{2}}\sum_{j\in H^{\prime}\cup\{j^{\prime}\}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}

Lemma
5.2

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}^{2}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}^{2}}\frac{20s+5}{\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert}\sum_{j\in R_{l}^{i}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}^{2}

Lemma
5.6

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}^{2}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}^{2}}\frac{s}{2^{2i}}\mathsf{Res}_{l}^{2}

Definition
5.3

By padding
\mathbf{x}
with zeros on
H\setminus H^{\prime}
and taking square roots, we get the desired result.
∎

Next, we apply an averaging argument to show that if we sum across all large level sets, we fit a constant fraction of columns all
d_{l}
with constant probability.

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose that
\lvert R_{l}^{\infty}\rvert\leq d_{l}/4
. Then with probability at least
1/960
, there is a set of size
F\subseteq[d_{l}]
such that
\lvert F\rvert\geq d_{l}/960
and

\sum_{j\in F}\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}}\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{g}\leq O(\sqrt{s})\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}}\cdot\mathsf{Res}_{l}

Proof.

By Lemma
5.6
, for a fixed level set
i
with
\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert\geq d_{l}/8\log_{2}d_{l}
, with probability at least
1/6
, we fit at least
\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert/20
columns with cost at most

O(\sqrt{s})\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}}\frac{\mathsf{Res}_{l}}{2^{i}}

each. Then, let
X_{i}
be the random variable that represents the number of such columns in
R_{l}^{i}
, and define

X\coloneqq\sum_{i:\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert\geq d_{l}/8\log_{2}d_{l}}X_{i}

Note then that

\E[X]\geq\sum_{i:\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert\geq d_{l}/8\log_{2}d_{l}}\frac{1}{6}\cdot\frac{1}{20}\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert\geq\frac{1}{6\cdot 20\cdot 4}d_{l}=\frac{1}{480}d_{l}

where the last inequality is by Lemma
5.5
.
Then by a standard averaging argument,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{480}d_{l}

\displaystyle\leq d_{l}\cdot\Pr\{X\geq d_{l}/960\}+\frac{d_{l}}{960}\Pr\{X<d_{l}/960\}

\displaystyle\leq d_{l}\cdot\Pr\{X\geq d_{l}/960\}+\frac{d_{l}}{960}

so
X
is at least
d_{l}/960
with probability at least
1/960
. Furthermore, the total cost of all of the columns which are fit well is at most

\sum_{i}O(\sqrt{s})\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}}\frac{\mathsf{Res}_{l}}{2^{i}}\cdot\lvert R_{l}^{i}\rvert\leq O(\sqrt{s})\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}}\cdot\mathsf{Res}_{l}.\qed

5.2.3
Proof of Theorem
1.5

We now give proofs for the various guarantees of our algorithm.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Note first that the algorithm decreases the size of
T_{l}
by a
(1-1/960)
factor at each iteration. Thus, the algorithm makes at most
L=O(\log d)
iterations of the outer loop. By Lemma
5.7
, we have a constant probability of success of choosing
d_{l}/960
columns such that the total cost is at most

O(\sqrt{s})\frac{\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}}\cdot\mathsf{Res}_{l}.

Since we repeat
O(\log L)=O(\log\log d)
times and use an
\mathsf{reg}_{g,t_{l}}
-approximate regression algorithm, we with probability at least
1-1/100L
, we find
d_{l}/960
columns
F_{l}\subseteq T_{l}
and corresponding coefficients
\mathbf{X}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{F_{l}}-\mathbf{A}|^{S_{l}}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{g}\leq O(\sqrt{s})\frac{\mathsf{reg}_{g,t_{l}}\cdot\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}}\cdot\mathsf{Res}_{l}.

Thus, our total cost is

\sum_{l=1}^{O(\log d)}O(\sqrt{s})\frac{\mathsf{reg}_{g,t_{l}}\cdot\mathsf{ati}_{g,s+1}}{\mathsf{lin}_{g}}\cdot\mathsf{Res}_{l}.

Finally, as argued in
[
SWZ19b
,
MW21
]
, we show that
\sum_{l}\mathsf{Res}_{l}=O(\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}\rVert_{g})
. Note that if a column
j
contributes to
\mathsf{Res}_{l}
, then it must be in the bottom
3/4
fraction of the
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}
in round
l
. Then since the bottom
1/960
fraction of
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}
is fitted and removed in each round,
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}
can only contribute to
\mathsf{Res}_{l}
in
O(1)
rounds. Thus, the sum is bounded by
O(1)\sum_{j}\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}=O(\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}\rVert_{g})
.

The total number of columns selected is
O(s\log d)
in each of the
O(\log d)
rounds, for a total of
O(s\log^{2}d)
.
∎

6
Huber Column Subset Selection

For the important case of the Huber loss, the result of Theorem
1.5
only yields a distortion of
\tilde{O}(k^{3/2})
, due to a
k
factor loss from the approximate triangle inequality term. We further optimize our argument specifically for the Huber loss and obtain a distortion of
O(k)
instead.

See
1.6

Our improvement comes from the following structural result, which yields Theorem
1.6
when combined with Theorem
A.3
:

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathbf{A}_{*}
denote the optimal rank
k
approximation to
\mathbf{A}
in the entrywise Huber norm. Then, there exists a set
S\subseteq[d]
of
O(k\log\log k)
columns of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times d}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{H}\leq O(d)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}_{*}\right\rVert_{H}.

Proof.

Let
S\subseteq[d]
be an
\ell_{2}
-well-conditioned coreset for the columns of
\mathbf{A}_{*}
, given by Corollary
3.5
. For each
j\notin S
, we let the
j
th column of
\mathbf{X}
be the coefficient vector for fitting
\mathbf{a}_{*}^{j}
by
\mathbf{A}_{*}|^{S}
.

Following
[
CW15a
, Lemma 37]
, we have that for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

H(\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2})\leq\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}H(\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\mathbf{x}_{i}^{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}H(\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}H(\mathbf{x}_{i})=\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{H}.

Then,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{H}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\mathbf{A}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\Delta})-(\mathbf{A}^{*}+\boldsymbol{\Delta})|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{H}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}-\boldsymbol{\Delta}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{H}

\displaystyle\leq O(1)(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}\right\rVert_{H}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{H})

so it suffices to bound
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{H}
. We have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{H}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Delta}|^{S}\mathbf{x}^{j})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(\left\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Delta}|^{S}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\mathbf{x}^{j}\right\rVert_{2})

Cauchy–Schwarz

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(\left\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Delta}|^{S}\right\rVert_{2})

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Delta}|^{S}\right\rVert_{H}

\displaystyle\leq O(1)\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}|^{S}\right\rVert_{H}

\displaystyle\leq O(d)\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}\right\rVert_{H}

as claimed.
∎

7
\ell_{p}
Column Subset Selection,
p>2

We improve the analysis of column subset selection algorithms which select more than
k
columns, by showing a randomized polynomial time algorithm for selecting
O(k\log d)
columns with a distortion of
O(k^{1/2-1/p})
. This improves the algorithms of
[
CGK+17
,
DWZ+19
]
in this regime and circumvents the lower bound of
\Omega(k^{1-1/p})
distortion for selecting exactly
k
columns.

7.1
Improved Existential Result for Bicriteria Column Subset Selection

Our main improvement comes from the following lemma, which is inspired by the techniques of
[
MW21
]
and our active
\ell_{p}
regression techniques with large distortion. Note that the proof techniques of
[
SWZ17
]
and
[
MW21
, Theorem 2.4]
do not apply for this result, since they use
p
-stable random variables, which do not exist for
p>2
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
2\leq p\leq\infty
. Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
\mathbf{A}_{*}
denote the optimal rank
k
approximation to
\mathbf{A}
in the entrywise
\ell_{p}
norm. Then, there exists a set
S\subseteq[d]
of
O(k)
columns of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}|^{S}\mathbf{R}\right\rVert_{p,p}\leq O(k^{1/2-1/p})\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}_{*}\right\rVert_{p,p}.

(12)

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{A}_{*}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
for some
\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
and
\mathbf{V}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
. Now let
\mathbf{w}
be the
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights of
\mathbf{V}
and let
\hat{\mathbf{X}}
minimize

\min_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\left\lVert(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\right\rVert_{p,2}

up to a factor of
2
. We have

\displaystyle\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}+\lVert\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}-\hat{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}

Theorem
2.4

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}+\lVert(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}-\hat{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\rVert_{p,2}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}+\lVert(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\rVert_{p,2}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt+\lVert(\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\rVert_{p,2}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}+3\lVert(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}-\mathbf{A})\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\rVert_{p,2}

near optimality

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}+3k^{1/2-1/p}\lVert\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}-\mathbf{A}\rVert_{p,p}

Lemma
2.5

\displaystyle=O(k^{1/2-1/p})\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}.

Thus, we have reduced the problem to an
\ell_{2}
problem, at a cost of
O(k^{1/2-1/p})
distortion. Lemma 27 of
[
CW15a
]
then shows that if
\mathbf{S}^{\top}
is an
\ell_{2}
sparsifier for
\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}
which samples
O(k)
columns (see
[
SWZ19a
, Lemma C.25]
, based on
[
BSS12
, Theorem 3.1]
), then a minimizer
\hat{\mathbf{U}}
of

\min_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\left\lVert(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{S}^{\top}\right\rVert_{p,2}

satisfies

\left\lVert(\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\right\rVert_{p,2}\leq 2\min_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}}\left\lVert(\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{X}\mathbf{V}^{\top})\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\right\rVert_{p,2}.

It follows that

\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}\leq O(k^{1/2-1/p})\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}\rVert_{p,p}.

Finally, note that
\hat{\mathbf{U}}
can be written as

\hat{\mathbf{U}}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{S}^{\top}(\mathbf{V}^{\top}\mathbf{W}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{S}^{\top})^{-}.

Thus, there exists an
O(k^{1/2-1/p})
-approximate solution with a left factor formed by
O(k)
columns of
\mathbf{A}
.
∎

With Lemma
7.1
in hand, we can now apply Theorem
A.3
to obtain the following:

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
2\leq p<\infty
. Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
k\geq 1
. There is an algorithm which outputs a subset
S\subseteq[d]
of
\lvert S\rvert=O(k\log d)
columns and
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times d}
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{p,p}

\displaystyle\leq O(k^{1/2-1/p})\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{p,p}.

We note that by setting
p=O(\log n)
, we also obtain a result for
p=\infty
.

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and let
k\geq 1
. There is an algorithm which outputs a subset
S\subseteq[d]
of
\lvert S\rvert=O(k\log d)
columns and
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times d}
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq O(k^{1/2})\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{\infty,\infty}.

7.2
Lower Bound

We give an impossibility result for
\ell_{\infty}
column subset selection, showing that our new result for
\ell_{\infty}
subset selection is approximately tight.

Our result is based on a variation on the ideas of Theorem 1.4 of
[
SWZ17
]
.

Definition 7.4

(Hard distribution)
.

Let
c\geq 1
be any constant and let
r=k^{c}
. We then define a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
(k+2^{r})\times r
matrix as follows. We let the first
k
rows have entries drawn independently from
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{r})
and scaled by
k
, and we let the last
2^{r}
rows be the
2^{r}
vectors in
\{\pm 1\}^{r}
.

We will argue that with high probability, no matrix in the column span of
r/2
columns of
\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}
can approximate
\mathbf{A}
by better than a
\sqrt{k}
factor. The optimal rank
k
approximation of any matrix drawn from the distribution in Definition
7.4
has
\ell_{\infty}
has cost at most
1
, by setting the rank
k
approximation to be the first
k
rows:

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\sim\mathcal{D}
for
\mathcal{D}
defined in Definition
7.4
. Then, with probability
1
,

\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq 1.

Furthermore, the addition of the
2^{r}
hypercube vectors to the matrix gives the following property:

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
S\subseteq[r]
. Then, for any
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times r}
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\geq\max_{j=1}^{r}\lVert\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{1}-1

Proof.

Let
j\in[r]
. Then, there exists a row
i
of
\mathbf{A}|^{S}
such that for each
j^{\prime}\in S
,
\mathbf{A}_{i,j^{\prime}}=\sgn(\mathbf{X}_{j^{\prime},j})
, since
\mathbf{A}
contains all sign vectors. Thus,

\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{A}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}_{j}=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in S}\mathbf{A}_{i,j^{\prime}}\mathbf{X}_{j^{\prime},j}=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in S}\sgn(\mathbf{X}_{j^{\prime},j})\mathbf{X}_{j^{\prime},j}=\left\lVert\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}_{j}\right\rVert_{1}.

On the other hand,
\mathbf{A}
has absolute value at most
1
on this coordinate, thus yielding the claim.
∎

With these insights in hand, the proof now essentially follows that of
[
SWZ17
, Theorem G.28]
; it is shown in
[
SWZ17
]
that if
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
fits the first
k
rows well in
\ell_{1}
norm, then it must satisfy
\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{1}=\Omega(k^{0.5-o(1)})
. Since we scale the first
k
rows by
k
, this means that we either have a high
\ell_{\infty}
cost in the first
k
rows, or a high
\ell_{\infty}
cost in the bottom
2^{r}
rows.

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,0.5)
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
r=\poly(k)
. Then, there exists a
(k+r)\times r
matrix
\mathbf{A}
such that

\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq 1

and for any
S\subseteq[r]
with
\lvert S\rvert\leq r/2
,

\min_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times r}}\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\geq\Omega(k^{0.5-\alpha}).

Proof.

The proof closely follows
[
SWZ17
, Theorem G.28]
. For
\mathbf{B}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{k\times s}
and scalars
\beta,\gamma>0
, we say the event
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{B},\beta,\gamma)
holds if

•

\left\lVert\mathbf{B}\right\rVert_{2}\leq O(\sqrt{s})

•

\mathbf{B}\mathbf{x}
has at most
O(k/\log k)
coordinates with absolute value at least
\Omega(1/\log k)
, whenever
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1}\leq O(k^{\gamma})
and
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(k^{-\beta})

(see
[
SWZ17
, Definition G.19]
). It is shown in
[
SWZ17
, Lemma G.20]
that if
k\leq s\leq\poly(k)
,
\beta>\gamma>0
, and
\beta+\gamma<1
, then
\Pr\{\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{B},\beta,\gamma)\}\geq 1-\exp(-\Theta(k))
. We will apply this to the first
k
rows
\mathbf{A}|_{[k]}
of
\mathbf{A}
scaled down by
k
, as well as to restrictions
\mathbf{A}|_{[k]}^{S}
of these rows to columns
S\subseteq[r]
.

It is shown in
[
SWZ17
, Claim G.29]
that for any
S\subseteq[r]
,

\Pr\left\{\mathcal{E}\left\lparen\frac{1}{k}\mathbf{A}|_{[k]}^{S},0.5+\alpha/2,0.5-\alpha\right\rparen\Big|\mathcal{E}\left\lparen\frac{1}{k}\mathbf{A}|_{[k]},0.5+\alpha/2,0.5-\alpha\right\rparen\right\}=1

We thus condition on
\mathcal{E}(\frac{1}{k}\mathbf{A}|_{[k]},0.5+\alpha/2,0.5-\alpha)
, which implies
\mathcal{E}(\frac{1}{k}\mathbf{A}|_{[k]}^{S},0.5+\alpha/2,0.5-\alpha)
for every
S\subseteq[r]
. Then by
[
SWZ17
, Lemma G.22]
, for any
S\subseteq[r]
of size at most
r/2
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Theta(rk))
, a constant fraction of the
r/2
remaining rows
l\in[r]\setminus S
satisfies that

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{S}}\left\lVert\frac{1}{k}\mathbf{A}_{[k]}|^{S}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{e}_{l}\right\rVert_{1}+\left\lVert\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{1}=\Omega(k^{0.5-\alpha})

By relating the
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{\infty}
norms up to a factor of
k
for the first term and by using Lemma
7.6
for the second term, this gives a lower bound of
\Omega(k^{0.5-\alpha})
on some entry of
\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}|^{S}\mathbf{X}
for any
\mathbf{X}
, for this fixed
S
. The failure rate of
\exp(-\Theta(rk))
is small enough for us to union bound over all choices of
S\subseteq[r]
of size at most
r/2
, thus giving the theorem.
∎

7.3
(1+\varepsilon)
-Approximate Bicriteria Algorithms

In a recent work of
[
BRW21
]
, additive approximations for low rank approximation in the entrywise
\ell_{p}
norm. In this section, we sharpen their argument using our well-conditioned spanning set result from Section
3
and combine it with our results from earlier in this section to obtain relative error
(1+\varepsilon)
approximations for this problem. While the main focus of the work of
[
BRW21
]
is on weighted low rank approximation, we specialize our discussion to standard low rank approximation. Our improvements apply to the weighted case as well.

While we show the overall idea and a complete proof of the key lemma for our improvement, we refer several lemmas which can be stated verbatim to
[
BRW21
]
.

7.3.1
Improved Algorithm for Additive Error

The algorithm of
[
BRW21
]
is based on an iterative process which updates approximations
\mathbf{x}^{j}_{(t)}
for each column
\mathbf{a}^{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
of
\mathbf{A}
. At each iteration
t
, the approximations
\mathbf{x}^{j}_{(t)}
for
j\in[d]
are updated along a single direction
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which is obtained by approximately solving for a vector witnessing the
p\mapsto 2
singular vector of a matrix. Then to update the approximation
\mathbf{x}^{j}_{(t)}
for each column, the algorithm proceeds as follows:

•

solve for the minimizer of
\eta\mapsto\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}-(\mathbf{x}^{j}_{(t)}+\eta\mathbf{z})\rVert_{p}

•

set
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbf{x}^{j}_{(t)}+\eta\mathbf{z}

•

solve for the minimizer of
\eta^{\prime}\mapsto\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}-\eta^{\prime}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\rVert_{p}

•

set
\mathbf{x}^{j}_{(t+1)}\leftarrow\eta^{\prime}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

Let
\mathbf{X}_{(t)}
denote the matrix with
\mathbf{x}^{j}_{(t)}
in its columns. The main argument of
[
BRW21
]
is based on a lemma stating that if the cost of the current approximation
\mathbf{X}_{(t)}
is larger than the cost of the optimal rank
k
solution
\mathbf{L}
, then there exists a good direction
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for updating each of the columns of
\mathbf{X}_{(t)}
. We will show that we can improve this lemma by using our Theorem
1.13
instead of
[
BRW21
, Lemma 9]
.

Following
[
BRW21
]
, we first restrict our attention to a set of good columns
\mathcal{G}\subseteq[d]
, where

\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\left\{j\in[d]:\left\lVert\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\right\}.

By combining with the fact that
\left\lVert\mathbf{L}\right\rVert_{p,p}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{p,p}
, it can easily be shown that the total mass on
[d]\setminus\mathcal{G}
is at most
\varepsilon\lVert\mathbf{A}\rVert_{p,p}^{p}
, and thus we do not need to fit these columns well (see
[
BRW21
, Lemma 6]
).

To analyze the algorithm, we follow
[
BRW21
]
and define the following functions:

\displaystyle f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}-\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle Q^{(t)}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{\langle\nabla f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}^{j}_{(t)}),\mathbf{z}\rangle^{2}}{(f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}^{j}_{(t)}))^{1-2/p}}

\displaystyle\delta^{*}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}}f_{j,p}(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j})}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{\mathcal{G}}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}}

\displaystyle\delta^{(t)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}}f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}_{(t)}^{j})}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{\mathcal{G}}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}}

The quantity
Q^{(t)}(\mathbf{z})
is the crucial quantity which represents the improvement that is possible, due to
[
BRW21
, Lemma 11]
. The quantities
\delta^{*}
and
\delta^{(t)}
represent the costs of the optimal and current approximations, respectively.

[
BRW21
, Lemma 13]
states the following:

Lemma 7.8

(Lemma 13,
[
BRW21
]
)
.

If
\delta^{(t)}>\delta^{*}
, then there exists
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}=1
such that

Q^{(t)}(\mathbf{z})\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{2/p}\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{\mathcal{G}}\rVert_{p,p}^{p}(\delta^{(t)}-\delta^{*})^{2}}{k^{2}}

We improve this to the following:

Lemma 7.9

(Improved Lemma 13,
[
BRW21
]
)
.

If
\delta^{(t)}>\delta^{*}
, then there exists
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\left\lVert\mathbf{z}\right\rVert_{p}=1
such that

Q^{(t)}(\mathbf{z})\geq\Omega(1)\frac{\varepsilon^{2/p}\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{\mathcal{G}}\rVert_{p,p}^{p}(\delta^{(t)}-\delta^{*})^{2}}{k\log\log k}

Proof.

We write
\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{V}^{\top}
using the decomposition given by Theorem
1.13
. Then for each
j\in[d]
, we can write
\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{v}^{j}
with
\lVert\mathbf{v}^{j}\rVert_{2}\leq O(1)\lVert\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{p}
. Furthermore, since
j\in\mathcal{G}
,
\lVert\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{p}\leq\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{p}/\varepsilon
. We then have, for
s=O(k\log\log k)
and each
j\in[d]
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{s}\langle\nabla f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}_{(t)}^{j}),\mathbf{u}^{i}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}_{(t)}^{j})-f_{j,p}(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}))^{2}}{\lVert\mathbf{v}^{j}\rVert_{2}^{2}}

Lemma 5,
[
BRW21
]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}_{(t)}^{j})-f_{j,p}(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}))^{2}}{O(1)\lVert\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}\rVert_{p}^{2}}

Theorem
1.13

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon^{2/p}\frac{(f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}_{(t)}^{j})-f_{j,p}(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}))^{2}}{O(1)\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{p}^{2}}

j\in\mathcal{G}

Dividing both sides by
f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}_{(t)}^{j})^{1-2/p}\leq\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{p}^{p-2}
and summing over
j\in\mathcal{G}
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{\langle\nabla f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}_{(t)}^{j}),\mathbf{u}^{i}\rangle^{2}}{f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}_{(t)}^{j})^{1-2/p}}

\displaystyle\geq\Omega(\varepsilon^{2/p})\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{(f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}_{(t)}^{j})-f_{j,p}(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}))^{2}}{\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{p}^{p}}

\displaystyle=\Omega(\varepsilon^{2/p})\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{\mathcal{G}}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}\cdot\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{(f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}_{(t)}^{j})-f_{j,p}(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}))^{2}}{\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{p}^{2p}}\cdot\frac{\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{p}^{p}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{\mathcal{G}}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}}

\displaystyle\geq\Omega(\varepsilon^{2/p})\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{\mathcal{G}}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}\cdot\left[\sum_{j\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{(f_{j,p}(\mathbf{x}_{(t)}^{j})-f_{j,p}(\mathbf{L}\mathbf{e}_{j}))}{\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{p}^{p}}\cdot\frac{\lVert\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{p}^{p}}{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{\mathcal{G}}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}}\right]^{2}

Jensen’s inequality

\displaystyle=\Omega(\varepsilon^{2/p})\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{\mathcal{G}}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}(\delta^{(t)}-\delta^{*})^{2}.

We conclude by averaging over
i\in[s]
.
∎

Given this improvement to the key lemma
[
BRW21
, Lemma 13]
, we obtain the following improvement to
[
BRW21
, Theorem 3]
, using the exact same proof:

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
2<p<\infty
, and let
k\geq 1
. Then, there exists an efficient algorithm that outputs a matrix
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
of rank at most
O(k(\log\log k)/\varepsilon^{1+2/p})
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{L}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}\leq\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{p,p}^{p}+\varepsilon\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}

7.3.2
Relative Error Approximation

We now compose the above additive error algorithm of Theorem
7.10
with our relative error algorithm of Theorem
7.2
to give the first relative error algorithm:

See
1.16

Proof.

We first apply Theorem
7.2
to find a matrix
\mathbf{B}
consisting of
r=O(k\log d)
columns such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{B}\right\rVert_{p,p}\leq O(k^{1/2-1/p})\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{p,p}

Now let
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{B}
. Then we apply Theorem
7.10
with rank parameter set to
r+k
and accuracy parameter set to
\varepsilon/k^{p/2-1}
. This produces an approximation
\mathbf{C}
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{B}^{\prime}-\mathbf{C}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{B}})\leq r+k}\lVert\mathbf{B}^{\prime}-\hat{\mathbf{B}}\rVert_{p,p}^{p}+\frac{\varepsilon}{k^{p/2-1}}\left\lVert\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{p,p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{p,p}^{p}+O(\varepsilon)\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{p,p}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq(1+O(\varepsilon))\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{p,p}^{p},

as desired.
∎

8
Online Coresets for
\ell_{p}
Subspace Approximation

For this section, we define the optimal value of the
\ell_{p}
subspace approximation problem as

\mathsf{OPT}_{p,k}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\min_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}^{p}=\min_{\rank(\mathbf{X})\leq k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{X})\right\rVert_{p,2}^{p}

We show the following theorem in this section:

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times d}
have entries bounded by
\Delta
, let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
, let
p\geq 1
a constant, and let
k
be a rank. Suppose that any submatrix of
\mathbf{A}
formed by consecutive rows has online condition number at most
\kappa
. There is an online coreset algorithm, Algorithm
2
, which stores at most

s=O\left\lparen\mathcal{S}\left\lparen k\cdot\min\{k^{2}\log k,k^{1\lor(p/2)}\}\log\mathcal{S}+\varepsilon^{\prime-2}\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\varepsilon^{-2}\varepsilon^{\prime-1}k^{2}\log\mathcal{S}\right\rparen\right\rparen

rows, where
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon^{(p+3)\cdot(1\lor(2/p))}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=O\left((k\log k+\log^{2}n)^{2}\log^{2}(n\Delta)\right)^{1\lor(p/2)}(\log^{2}k+\log^{2}\log n)(\log n)\log\frac{n}{\delta}

(13)

\displaystyle=k^{2\lor p}\cdot(\log(n\Delta/\delta))^{O(p)}

If
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the resulting sampling matrix, then with probability at least
1-\delta
, simultaneously for all
i\in[n]
and
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
, we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{S}_{i}\mathbf{A}_{i}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}^{p}=(1\pm\varepsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}_{i}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}^{p}

As we show in Section
8.1
, this immediately gives the following for real valued matrices:

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
, let
p\geq 1
a constant, and let
k
be a rank. Suppose that any submatrix of
\mathbf{A}
formed by consecutive rows has online condition number at most
\kappa
. There is an online coreset algorithm, Algorithm
2
, which stores at most

s=O\left\lparen\mathcal{S}\left\lparen k\cdot\min\{k^{2}\log k,k^{1\lor(p/2)}\}\log\mathcal{S}+\varepsilon^{\prime-2}\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\varepsilon^{-2}\varepsilon^{\prime-1}k^{2}\log\mathcal{S}\right\rparen\right\rparen

rows, where
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon^{(p+3)\cdot(1\lor(2/p))}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=O\left((k\log k+\log^{2}n)^{2}\log^{2}(n\kappa^{\mathsf{OL}})\right)^{1\lor(p/2)}(\log^{2}k+\log^{2}\log n)(\log n)\log\frac{n}{\delta}

\displaystyle=k^{2\lor p}\cdot(\log(n\kappa^{\mathsf{OL}}/\delta))^{O(p)}

If
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the resulting sampling matrix, then with probability at least
1-\delta
, simultaneously for all
i\in[n]
and
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
, we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{S}_{i}\mathbf{A}_{i}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}^{p}=(1\pm\varepsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{A}_{i}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}^{p}

Proof Sketch of Theorem
8.1
.

The overall approach is based on showing that sensitivity sampling can be made to work online. We show in Section
8.2
that a constant factor bicriteria solution can be found in an online manner. In Section
8.3
, we show how to use this online bicriteria solution to estimate sensitivities. Finally, we show in Section
8.4
that sampling by using these weights yields a strong coreset.

By Lemma
8.16
, Algorithm
3
returns a sensitivity upper bound with constant probability for each
i\in[n]
. We may then repeat
O(\log(n/\delta))
times so that the sum of the repetitions as taken in Line
2
yields a valid sensitivity overestimate simultaneously for every
i\in[n]
with probability at least
1-\delta
, by a union bound. Furthermore, again by Lemma
8.16
, the total sensitivity is as claimed in Equation (
13
).

Lemma
8.21
shows the number of samples required to reduce the problem of finding strong coresets for
\mathbf{A}
to finding strong coresets for a projection of
\mathbf{A}
onto a lower dimensional space of dimension
O(k/\varepsilon^{\prime})
. Lemma
8.22
shows the number of samples required to obtain a strong coreset in the lower dimensional space.
∎

Algorithm 2

Online Sensitivity Approximation

input:
Stream
\mathbf{A}
, rank
k
, total online sensitivity upper bound
\mathcal{S}
.

output:
Online coreset for
\ell_{p}
subspace approximation.

1:

Obtain online rank
2k
sensitivity overestimates from
O(\log(n/\delta))
independent copies of Algorithm
3

2:

For
i\in[n]
, let
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}^{\mathsf{OL}}
be the sum of the overestimates across the
O(\log(n/\delta))
copies

3:

Use
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}^{\mathsf{OL}}
and
\mathcal{S}
to sample rows as done in Theorem
B.9

8.1
Reduction to Integer Matrices

We first reduce the case of real-valued matrices to the case of integer matrices by rounding the input matrix. This allows us to control the conditioning of the solution in a simple way, since integer matrices have bounded condition number
[
CW09
]
. Let
\tilde{\lambda}^{p,\mathsf{OL}}
be a lower bound on

\lambda^{p,\mathsf{OL}}\coloneqq\min_{i\in[n]:\rank(\mathbf{A}_{i})>k}\min_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}_{i}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}^{p}.

That is,
\lambda^{p,\mathsf{OL}}
is the smallest nonzero cost of
\mathbf{A}_{i}
for any
i\in[n]
. Note that by the equivalence of
\ell_{p}
norms, this quantity is related up to a factor of
\poly(n)
with
\lambda^{\mathsf{OL}}\coloneqq\lambda^{2,\mathsf{OL}}
. Then, we may round each entry of
\mathbf{A}
to the nearest integer multiple of
\varepsilon n^{-1/p}d^{-1/2}(\tilde{\lambda}^{p,\mathsf{OL}})^{1/p}
to obtain a matrix
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
, so that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{p,2}^{p}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left\lvert\mathbf{A}[i,j]-\mathbf{A}^{\prime}[i,j]\right\rvert^{2}\right]^{p/2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\varepsilon^{2}n^{-2/p}(\tilde{\lambda}^{p,\mathsf{OL}})^{2/p}\right]^{p/2}\leq\varepsilon^{p}\tilde{\lambda}^{p,\mathsf{OL}}.

Then, for all
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}=\left\lVert\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}\pm\varepsilon\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{p,2}\subseteq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}\pm\varepsilon\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}

which implies that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}^{p}=(1\pm\varepsilon)^{p}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{P}_{F})\right\rVert_{p,2}^{p}

for all
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
, so it suffices to solve our problem on
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
, which, up to a scaling, is an integer matrix with entries bounded by

\Delta=\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{\infty}}{\varepsilon n^{-1/p}d^{-1/2}(\tilde{\lambda}^{p,\mathsf{OL}})^{1/p}}\leq\poly(n,\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\right\rVert_{\infty}/\tilde{\lambda}^{p,\mathsf{OL}})\leq\poly(n,\kappa^{\mathsf{OL}}).

8.2
Constant Factor Approximation

We first obtain a constant factor bicriteria approximation. For this, our approach is to adapt Theorem 4.1 of
[
FKW21
]
, which shows that a Lewis weight sample from
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{G}^{\top}
for a Gaussian matrix
\mathbf{G}
with
\tilde{O}(k)
columns yields rows whose span contains an
O(1)
-approximate solution. Although
[
FKW21
]
only states the result for
p=1
, we show that the same proof and conclusion holds for all
p\geq 1
. For our online implementation, we will replace Lewis weights with online Lewis weights
[
WY23
]
. Furthermore, we replace the use of a Gaussian matrix
\mathbf{G}
with an
\ell_{2}
subspace embedding with integer entries, so that the sketch also has integer entries.

8.2.1
Dimension Reduction

We first replace the use of a dense Gaussian matrix in
[
FKW21
]
with an integer subspace embedding, so that the resulting matrix is integer. One possibility
10
10

10

There are many possible alternatives here, for example a dense sign matrix, but we choose SRHT since the results we need are stated and proven in the existing literature.
is the subsampled randomized Hadamard transform (SRHT), which has the following guarantees:

Definition 8.3

(Subsampled Randomized Hadamard Transform (SRHT), Definition 1.2,
[
BG13
]
)
.

Fix
r
an integer and
n
a power of
2
with
r<n
. An SRHT matrix is an
r\times n
matrix
\mathbf{S}=\sqrt{n/r}\cdot\mathbf{R}\mathbf{H}\mathbf{D}
, where
\mathbf{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a random Rademacher diagonal matrix,
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a Walsh–Hadamard matrix, and
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times n}
