\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq C^{i/p}\varepsilon^{\beta_{i}/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{S}_{i,i}-1)\Delta_{i}\right\rvert\leq O(\varepsilon^{\beta_{i+1}})(\mathsf{OPT}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b})^{p}+\mathsf{OPT}(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A},\mathbf{S}\mathbf{b})^{p}).

By a union bound, all
I
of these events occurs with probability
1-I\delta
. Now assume that
\mathsf{OPT}(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A},\mathbf{S}\mathbf{b})=O(\mathsf{OPT}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{b}))
. Then, we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{S}}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq(1+C^{i}\varepsilon^{\beta_{i}})\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\implies\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq C^{i/p}\varepsilon^{\beta_{i}/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{S}_{i,i}-1)\Delta_{i}\right\rvert\leq O(\varepsilon^{\beta_{i+1}})\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

Furthermore, by Lemma
11.7
, we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{S}}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq(1+C^{i}\varepsilon^{\beta_{i}})\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\implies\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{S}}^{*}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq O(C^{i/p}\varepsilon^{\beta_{i}/p})\mathsf{OPT}

so by Lemma
11.8
, we then have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{S}}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq(1+C^{i}\varepsilon^{\beta_{i}})\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\implies\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{S}}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq(1+C^{i+1}\varepsilon^{\beta_{i+1}})\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

for large enough
C
. We may now follow the chain of implications to conclude that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{S}}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq(1+C\varepsilon^{\beta_{1}})\mathsf{OPT}^{p}=(1+C\varepsilon^{(p-1)/p})\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

\displaystyle\implies~

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{S}}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}\leq(1+C^{I}\varepsilon^{\beta_{I}})\mathsf{OPT}^{p}=(1+\varepsilon\poly\log(1/\varepsilon))\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

The hypothesis of the implication is shown by
[
MMWY22
, Theorem 3.4]
.
∎

12
Improved Distortion Bounds for Close Points

In this section, we prove Theorem
11.9
(restated below), which gives an improved bound on the distortion of points
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}
that are near the optimal regression solution
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}
.

See
11.9

12.1
Set Up

We split the sum in (
17
) into two parts: the part that is bounded by the Lewis weights of
\mathbf{A}
, and the part that is not. To this end, define a threshold

\tau_{i}\coloneqq\gamma\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1}}{\varepsilon^{p}}\mathbf{w}_{i}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

and define the set of “good” entries
G\subseteq[n]
as

G=\left\{i\in[n]:\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert\leq\tau_{i}\right\}

We then bound

\displaystyle\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{l}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{l-1}\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}\sigma_{i}\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{l}

\displaystyle+2^{l-1}\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus G}\sigma_{i}\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{l}

using the relaxed triangle inequality, and separately estimate each term. We can think of the first term as the “sensitivity” term, where each term in the sum is bounded by the Lewis weights of
\mathbf{A}
, and the latter term as the “outlier” term, where each term in the sum is much larger than the corresponding Lewis weights.

12.2
Estimates on the Outlier Term

We first bound the outlier terms, which is much easier.

Lemma 12.1
.

With probability
1
, we have that

\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus G}\left\lvert\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert\leq O(\varepsilon)\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

Proof.

For each
i\in[n]\setminus G
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\in\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert\pm\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}](i)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\in\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert\pm\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{w}_{i}^{1/p}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle\in\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert\pm\gamma^{1/p}\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{1/2-1/p}\mathbf{w}_{i}^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}

Lemma
2.2

\displaystyle\in\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert\pm\varepsilon\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert

\displaystyle i\in[n]\setminus G

Thus,

\left\lvert\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert\leq O(\varepsilon)\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert^{p}

so

\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus G}\left\lvert\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}O(\varepsilon)\left\lvert[\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}](i)\right\rvert^{p}=O(\varepsilon)\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.\qed

12.3
Estimates on the Sensitivity Term

Next, we estimate the sensitivity term,

\E\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}\sigma_{i}\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{l}.

To estimate this moment, we obtain a subgaussian tail bound via the tail form of Dudley’s entropy integral, and then integrate it.

12.3.1
Change of Density

We follow the Lewis weight chaining argument of
[
MMWY22
]
and
[
LT91
]
. We start with a change of density using the Lewis weights so that

\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}\sigma_{i}\Delta_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert=\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}\mathbf{w}_{i}\sigma_{i}\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert

where we define
\bar{\Delta}_{i}
as the corresponding versions of
\Delta_{i}
reweighted by
\mathbf{w}_{i}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}

Note that
\left\lvert\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert
is bounded over all
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}
, since

\displaystyle\left\lvert\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}+\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}

(19)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p-1}\left\lparen\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}+\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*})](i)\right\rvert^{p}\right\rparen+\left\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b})](i)\right\rvert^{p}

\displaystyle\leq(2^{p-1}+1)\tau+\gamma\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq(2^{p-1}+1+\varepsilon^{p})\tau=O(\tau)

using Lemma
2.2
, where

\tau\coloneqq\gamma\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1}}{\varepsilon^{p}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

12.3.2
Bounding Low-Sensitivity Entries

We now separately handle entries
i\in G
with small Lewis weight. To do this end, define

J\coloneqq\left\{i\in G:\mathbf{w}_{i}\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{p+1}}{\gamma n\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1}}\right\}.

We then bound the mass on the complement of
J
:

Lemma 12.2
.

For all
\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\cdot\mathsf{OPT}
, we have that

\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lvert\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert\leq O(\varepsilon)\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

Proof.

Note that for each
i\in[n]\setminus G
,

\mathbf{w}_{i}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\tau n}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

For each
i\in G\setminus J
, we use (
19
) to bound
\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})
by
O(\tau)
. Summing the bounds yields the result.
∎

12.3.3
Bounding High-Sensitivity Entries: Gaussian Processes

In order to obtain tail bounds, we first use Panchenko’s lemma to bound a Gaussian process instead of a Rademacher process.

Lemma 12.3

(Lemma 1,
[
Pan03
]
)
.

Let
X,Y
be random variables such that

\E[\Phi(X)]\leq\E[\Phi(Y)]

for every increasing convex function
\Phi
. If

\Pr\left\{Y\geq t\right\}\leq c_{1}\exp(-c_{2}t^{\alpha})\qquad\mbox{for all $t\geq 0$},

for some
c_{1},\alpha\geq 1
and
c_{2}>0
, then

\Pr\left\{X\geq t\right\}\leq c_{1}\exp(1-c_{2}t^{\alpha})\qquad\mbox{for all $t\geq 0$}.

Let
\Phi
be any increasing convex function. Since
\mathbf{w}_{i}\leq Wd/n
, we bound

\E\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\Phi\left\lparen\left\lvert\sum_{i\in J}\sigma_{i}\mathbf{w}_{i}\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert\right\rparen\leq O(1)\sqrt{\frac{W}{n}}\cdot\E\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\Phi\left\lparen\left\lvert\sum_{i\in J}\sigma_{i}\sqrt{d\mathbf{w}_{i}}\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert\right\rparen

by the Rademacher contraction theorem
[
LT91
, Theorem 4.12]
. Then by a Gaussian comparison theorem
[
LT91
, Equation 4.8]
, we may bound the above by

O(1)\sqrt{\frac{W}{n}}\cdot\E\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\Phi\left\lparen\left\lvert\sum_{i\in J}g_{i}\sqrt{d\mathbf{w}_{i}}\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert\right\rparen,

where the Rademacher variables
\sigma_{i}
have been replaced by Gaussian variables
g_{i}
. Thus by Lemma
12.3
, it suffices to obtain tail bounds for

O(1)\sqrt{\frac{W}{n}}\cdot\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in J}g_{i}\sqrt{d\mathbf{w}_{i}}\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert,

(20)

We can now appeal to the theory of Gaussian processes to bound this quantity.

Let

Z\coloneqq O(1)\frac{\gamma^{1/2}}{\varepsilon^{p/2-1}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

be a normalizing quantity and define a Gaussian process by

G_{\mathbf{x}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{Z}\sum_{i\in J}g_{i}\sqrt{d\mathbf{w}_{i}}\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})

with pseudo-metric

d_{G}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\coloneqq\left\lparen\E_{g}\left\lvert G_{\mathbf{x}}-G_{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}\right\rvert^{2}\right\rparen^{1/2}=\Theta(1)\frac{1}{Z}\left\lparen\sum_{i\in J}d\mathbf{w}_{i}(\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})-\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}))^{2}\right\rparen^{1/2}

As we will see later,
Z
is chosen to scale the Gaussian process to the scale of the ball
\left\{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}\right\}
.

12.3.4
Estimates on the Gaussian Process Geometry

Using the sensitivity bound of (
19
), we obtain a bound on the pseudo-metric
d_{G}
, which improves over the bound of
[
MMWY22
]
by introducing a dependence on
\gamma
.

Lemma 12.4
.

For
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\left\{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}\right\}
, we have that

d_{G}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\cdot O(\sqrt{d})(\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1})^{1/2-1/p}\left\lVert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]\mid_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}

Proof.

For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we have by convexity for
p>1
that

\left\lvert a\right\rvert^{p}-\left\lvert b\right\rvert^{p}\leq p(\left\lvert a\right\rvert^{p-1}+\left\lvert b\right\rvert^{p-1})\left\lvert\left\lvert a\right\rvert-\left\lvert b\right\rvert\right\rvert\leq p(\left\lvert a\right\rvert^{p-1}+\left\lvert b\right\rvert^{p-1})\left\lvert a-b\right\rvert

Then by applying the above to
a=[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b})](i)
and
b=[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)
, we have

\displaystyle Z^{2}d_{G}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{d})\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}(\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})-\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{d})\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lparen\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b})](i)\rvert^{p}-\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\rvert^{p}\right\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{d})\left\lVert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]\mid_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lparen\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b})](i)\rvert\lor\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\rvert\right\rparen^{2p-2}.

Now using (
19
), we have

\left\lparen\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b})](i)\rvert^{p}\lor\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\rvert^{p}\right\rparen\leq O(\gamma)\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1}}{\varepsilon^{p}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}

so

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lparen\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b})](i)\rvert^{2p-2}+\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\rvert^{2p-2}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O(1)\left\lparen\gamma\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1}}{\varepsilon^{p}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\right\rparen^{1-2/p}\sum_{i\in J}\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lparen\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b})](i)\rvert^{p}+\lvert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b})](i)\rvert^{p}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle O(1)\left\lparen\gamma\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1}}{\varepsilon^{p}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\right\rparen^{1-2/p}(\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p})

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O(1)\left\lparen\gamma\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1}}{\varepsilon^{p}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\right\rparen^{1-2/p}(\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}+\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{p}^{p})

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O(1)\left\lparen\gamma\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1}}{\varepsilon^{p}}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}\right\rparen^{1-2/p}\mathsf{OPT}^{p}.

Altogether,

\displaystyle d_{G}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{d})\left\lVert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]\mid_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}\frac{1}{Z}\left\lparen\gamma\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1}}{\varepsilon^{p}}\right\rparen^{1/2-1/p}\mathsf{OPT}^{p-1}

\displaystyle\leq O(\sqrt{d})\left\lVert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]\mid_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lparen\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1}\right\rparen^{1/2-1/p}\cdot\frac{1}{\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}

as claimed.
∎

Similarly, we obtain bounds on the
d_{G}
-diameter of the ball
\left\{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}\right\}
.

Lemma 12.5
.

Let
T=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}\right\}
. Then,

\diam(T)=\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in T}d_{G}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\leq O(\sqrt{d})(\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1})^{1/2-1/p}

Proof.

This follows from applying Lemma
12.4
and then a sensitivity bound (Lemma
2.2
) to bound

\left\lVert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]\mid_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}.\qed

12.3.5
Dudley’s Entropy Integral

We will obtain tail bounds on (
20
) via the following tail bound version of Dudley’s inequality:

Theorem 12.6

(Theorem 8.1.6,
[
Ver18
]
)
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
be a Gaussian process with pseudo-metric
d_{X}(s,t)\coloneqq\left\lVert X_{s}-X_{t}\right\rVert_{2}
. Let
E(T,d_{X},u)
denote the minimal number of
d_{X}
-balls of radius
u
required to cover
T
. Then, for every
z\geq 0
, we have that

\Pr\left\{\sup_{t\in T}X_{t}\geq C\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log E(T,d_{X},u)}~du+z\cdot\diam(T)\right]\right\}\leq 2\exp(-z^{2})

We now calculate Dudley’s entropy integral, using metric entropy bounds from
[
WY23
]
.

Lemma 12.7
.

Let
T=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}\right\}
. Then,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log E(T,d_{G},u)}~du\leq O(1)\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2}(\log(d\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}))^{2}(\log n)\right]^{1/2}

Proof.

Let
B_{p}(\mathbf{A})\coloneqq\colspan(\mathbf{A})\cap B_{p}
and note that the supremum we wish to bound is over the set
T\coloneqq O(\gamma^{1/p})\mathsf{OPT}\cdot B_{p}(\mathbf{A})
.

Let
\bar{\mathbf{w}}=\mathbf{w}/\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}
be the normalized one-sided Lewis weights and define the norm

\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\bar{\mathbf{w}},q}\coloneqq\left\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\mathbf{w}}_{i}\left\lvert\mathbf{y}_{i}\right\rvert^{q}\right\rparen^{1/q}=\frac{1}{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{1/q}}\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{1/q}\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{q}

As reasoned in
[
WY23
]
, we have that for
q=O(\log n)
,

\left\lVert[\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]_{J}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(1)\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{\bar{\mathbf{w}},q}

since
\mathbf{w}_{i}\geq 1/\poly(n)
for
i\in J
. Then, we can bound the metric entropy of
T
with respect to
d_{G}
-balls of radius
t
using the above by

\displaystyle\log E(O(\gamma^{1/p})\mathsf{OPT}\cdot B_{p}(\mathbf{A}),d_{G},t)

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\log E(O(\gamma^{1/p})\mathsf{OPT}\cdot B_{p}(\mathbf{A}),\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\cdot)\rVert_{\bar{\mathbf{w}},q},(\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT})t/O(\sqrt{d})(\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1})^{1/2-1/p})

Lemma
12.4

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\log E(B_{p}(\mathbf{A}),\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\cdot)\rVert_{\bar{\mathbf{w}},q},t/O(\sqrt{d})(\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1})^{1/2-1/p})

scaling

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\log E(\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{1/p}B_{p}(\mathbf{A}),\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\cdot)\rVert_{\bar{\mathbf{w}},q},\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{1/p}t/O(\sqrt{d})(\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2-1})^{1/2-1/p})

scaling

Now since
\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\bar{\mathbf{w}},p}=\left\lVert\mathbf{W}^{1/p}\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}/\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{1/p}
, we have that
B_{p}(\mathbf{A})\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{1/p}=B_{\bar{\mathbf{w}},p}(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A})
, where

B_{\bar{\mathbf{w}},p}(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A})=\left\{\mathbf{y}:\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{\bar{\mathbf{w}},p}\leq 1\right\}\cap\colspan(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A})

so the above metric entropy is equal to

\log E(B_{\bar{\mathbf{w}},p}(\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}\mathbf{A}),\lVert\mathbf{W}^{-1/p}(\cdot)\rVert_{\bar{\mathbf{w}},q},t/O(\sqrt{d}/\sqrt{\lVert\mathbf{w}\rVert_{1}})(\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2})^{1/2-1/p})

Then, the net bounds in Corollary B.9 of
[
WY23
]
show that this is at most

O\left\lparen\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2}}{\alpha t^{2}}q\right\rparen=O\left\lparen\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2}}{\alpha t^{2}}\log n\right\rparen.

Dudley’s entropy integral then gives a bound of

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log E(O(\gamma^{1/p})\mathsf{OPT}\cdot B_{p}(\mathbf{A}),d_{G},t)}

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\diam(T)}\sqrt{\log E(O(\gamma^{1/p})\mathsf{OPT}\cdot B_{p}(\mathbf{A}),d_{G},t)}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O(1)\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{d\log\frac{n}{t}}~dt+O(\alpha^{-1/2})\int_{1}^{\diam(T)}\frac{\sqrt{\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2}\log n}}{t}~dt

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O(\alpha^{-1/2})\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2}(\log\diam(T))^{2}(\log n)\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O(\alpha^{-1/2})\left[\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2}(\log(d\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}))^{2}(\log n)\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\text{Lemma \ref{lem:diam}}\qed

As a result of the above calculations, we have the following tail bound:

Corollary 12.8
.

There is
C=\Theta(1)
such that for every
z\geq 0
, we have that

\Pr\left\{\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in J}\frac{1}{Z}g_{i}\sqrt{d\mathbf{w}_{i}}\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert\geq C\alpha^{-1/2}\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/4}\left\lparen\left[(\log(d\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}))^{2}(\log n)\right]^{1/2}+z\right\rparen\right\}\leq 2\exp(-z^{2})

Proof.

This follows from Dudley’s tail bound in Theorem
12.6
, the entropy calculation in Lemma
12.7
, and the diameter calculations in Lemma
12.5
.
∎

12.3.6
Moment Bounds

With tail bounds in place, we estimate the moments of (
20
).

Lemma 12.9
.

\E\left[\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in J}\frac{1}{Z}g_{i}\sqrt{d\mathbf{w}_{i}}\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{l}\right]\leq O(\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/4}\alpha^{-1/2})^{l}\cdot\left[((\log(d\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}))(\log n)^{1/2})^{l+1}+O(l)^{l/2}\right]

Proof.

Let

\Lambda=\frac{1}{C\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/4}}\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in J}\frac{1}{Z}g_{i}\sqrt{d\mathbf{w}_{i}}\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert

and
z_{0}=(\log(d\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}))(\log n)^{1/2}
. We have that

\displaystyle\E[\Lambda^{l}]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}l\cdot z^{l}\Pr(\Lambda\geq z)~dz

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2z_{0}}l\cdot z^{l}\Pr(\Lambda\geq z)~dz+\int_{2z_{0}}^{\infty}l\cdot z^{l}\Pr(\Lambda\geq z)~dz

\displaystyle\leq(2z_{0})^{l+1}+\int_{2z_{0}}^{\infty}l\cdot z^{l}\Pr(\Lambda\geq z/2+z_{0})~dz

\displaystyle\leq(2z_{0})^{l+1}+2l\int_{2z_{0}}^{\infty}z^{l}\exp(-z^{2}/4)~dz

Corollary
12.8

\displaystyle\leq(2z_{0})^{l+1}+l\cdot O(l)^{l/2}.\qed

We are now ready to prove Theorem
11.9
.

Proof of Theorem
11.9
.

We split the sum into the sensitivity term and outlier term, as discussed in Section
12.1
. To bound the outlier term, we use the triangle inequality and Lemma
12.1
to bound by
\left\lparen O(\varepsilon)\mathsf{OPT}\right\rparen^{l}
. To bound the sensitivity term, we split the sum into the the indices in
J
and those outside of
J
. For those outside of
J
, we use Lemma
12.2
to get a bound of
\left\lparen O(\varepsilon)\mathsf{OPT}\right\rparen^{l}
. For those in
J
, we have

\displaystyle O\left\lparen\sqrt{\frac{W}{n}}\right\rparen^{l}Z^{l}\E\left[\sup_{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}}\left\lvert\sum_{i\in J}\frac{1}{Z}g_{i}\sqrt{d\mathbf{w}_{i}}\bar{\Delta}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert^{l}\right]

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle O\left\lparen\sqrt{\frac{W}{n}}\right\rparen^{l}Z^{l}O(\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/4}\alpha^{-1/2})^{l}\cdot\left[((\log(d\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}))(\log n)^{1/2})^{l+1}+O(l)^{l/2}\right]

Lemma
12.9

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\left[O(1)\frac{W}{n}\frac{\gamma}{\alpha\varepsilon^{p-2}}\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2}\left\lparen[(\log(d\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}))^{2}(\log n)]^{1+1/l}+l\right\rparen\right]^{l/2}(\mathsf{OPT}^{p})^{l}.

Now note that

\varepsilon=\left[O(1)\frac{W}{n}\frac{\gamma}{\alpha\varepsilon^{p-2}}\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2}\left\lparen(\log(d\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}))^{2}(\log n)]^{1+1/l}+l\right\rparen\right]^{1/2}

since this rearranges to

\varepsilon^{p}=O(1)\frac{W}{n}\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}^{p/2}\left\lparen[(\log(d\left\lVert\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{1}))^{2}(\log n)]^{1+1/l}+l\right\rparen.

This shows the desired result.
∎

13
Nearly Optimal Lower Bound for Active
\ell_{p}
Regression

In this section, we obtain a tight lower bound for active
\ell_{p}
regression for
p>2
, up to polylogarithmic factors.

We will need the following coding theory theorem, which was also used in
[
LWY21
,
MMWY22
]
to construct hard instances for linear algebraic problems.

Theorem 13.1

(
[
PTB13
]
)
.

For any
q\geq 1
and
d=2^{k}-1
for some integer
k
, there exists a set
S\subset\{\pm 1\}^{d}
and a constant
C_{q}
depending on
q
such that
\left\lvert S\right\rvert=d^{q}
and for every
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in S
with
\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}
, we have that
\left\lvert\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq C_{q}\sqrt{d}
.

We present our main lower bound, which generalizes a lower bound of
[
CSS21a
]
to
d
dimensions for linear regression.

Theorem 13.2
.

Let
p>2
. Suppose that a randomized algorithm solves the
\ell_{p}
regression up to a relative error of
(1+\varepsilon/3)
and queries
m
entries in expectation and is correct with probability at least
0.99
. Then,
m=\Omega(d^{p/2}/\varepsilon^{p-1})
.

Proof.

By Yao’s minimax principle, we may assume that the algorithm is deterministic with correctness probability at least
0.99
over a distributional hard instance. Let
S
be the set given by Theorem
13.1
with
q=p/2
. Set
n=s\cdot d^{p/2}
for
s=c/\varepsilon^{p-1}
with
c
a sufficiently small constant to be determined. Then, we take our matrix to be the
n\times d
matrix formed by taking
s
copies of each of the
d^{p/2}
vectors in
S
. Furthermore, we take our target vector
\mathbf{b}
to be the zero vector with probability
1/2
and
d\cdot\mathbf{e}_{I}
with probability
1/2
, where
I\sim[n]
is a uniformly random index and
\mathbf{e}_{i}
is the
i
th standard basis vector for
i\in[n]
.

Call the deterministic algorithm
\mathcal{A}
. Suppose for contradiction that
m\leq n/100
. Consider the sequence of entries of
\mathbf{b}
read by
\mathcal{A}
when
\mathbf{b}=0
. Note that this sequence is of length at most
2m
, since otherwise
\mathcal{A}
already reads more than
m
entries in expectation. Furthermore,
\mathcal{A}
must output
\mathbf{x}=0
as the solution if it reads a sequence of
2m
entries of zeros, since otherwise
\mathcal{A}
cannot achieve any relative error. Then since
\mathcal{A}
is deterministic,
\mathcal{A}
will always output
\mathbf{x}=0
if it reads
2m
entries of zeros.

On the other hand, suppose that
\mathbf{b}=d\cdot\mathbf{e}_{I}
for
I\sim[n]
. We first upper bound the optimal cost. If we choose
\mathbf{x}=\varepsilon\cdot\mathbf{a}_{I}
, then for the nonzero row of
\mathbf{b}
, we pay a cost of

(d-\varepsilon\cdot\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}\right\rangle)^{p}=(1-\varepsilon)^{p}d^{p}\leq(1-\varepsilon)d^{p}.

For the other rows of
\mathbf{A}
corresponding to copies of
\mathbf{a}_{I}
, we pay a cost of

s\cdot(\varepsilon\cdot\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{I}\right\rangle)^{p}=\frac{c}{\varepsilon^{p-1}}\cdot\varepsilon^{p}\cdot d^{p}=c\varepsilon d^{p}.

For all other rows of
\mathbf{A}
for
\mathbf{a}_{j}\neq\mathbf{a}_{I}
, we pay a cost of

s\cdot d^{p/2}\cdot(\varepsilon\cdot\left\langle\mathbf{a}_{I},\mathbf{a}_{j}\right\rangle)^{p}=\frac{c}{\varepsilon^{p-1}}\cdot\varepsilon^{p}\cdot d^{p/2}\cdot C_{q}^{p}d^{p/2}=cC_{q}^{p}\varepsilon d^{p}.

Thus, if we choose
c\leq\min\{C_{q}^{p},1\}/3
, then the total cost is at most

(1-\varepsilon)d^{p}+c\varepsilon d^{p}+cC_{q}^{p}\varepsilon d^{p}\leq(1-\varepsilon/3)d^{p}.

Now note that if
\mathbf{b}=d\cdot\mathbf{e}_{I}
, then the probability that
I
lands on one of the
2m
entries read by
\mathcal{A}
when
\mathbf{b}=0
is at most
2m/n\leq 1/50
. Thus, with probability at least
1-1/50
,
\mathcal{A}
outputs
\mathbf{x}=0
on this instance, which has a cost of
d^{p}
. By the above calculation, this fails to be a
(1+\varepsilon/3)
-approximate solution, which contradicts the guarantee of
\mathcal{A}
. We thus conclude that
m\geq n/100=\Omega(d^{p/2}/\varepsilon^{p-1})
.
∎

14
Active Regression with Large Distortion

Our results for active
\ell_{p}
regression have assumed that
p
is a fixed constant to obtain algorithms reading only
\poly(d)
entries. However, for
p
very large, an exponential dependence on
p
is intractable, and our argument gives nothing for the important case of
p=\infty
. In this section, we show how to handle
\ell_{p}
norms even for large
p
by reading only
\poly(d)
entries, where the degree of the polynomial does not depend on
p
, by trading off for a
\poly(d)
factor distortion. Our techniques yield many other results on dimension reduction in the large distortion regime.

14.1
Reduction to Subspace Embeddings

Our algorithms for this section are a generalization of the observation that subspace embeddings which satisfy a “no expansion” condition in expectation for any vector yields an active regression algorithm. This is used in
[
MMWY22
]
to obtain an initial constant factor approximation for active
\ell_{p}
regression. We generalize this to the following:

Lemma 14.1
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
and
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{X}
and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{Y}
be two norms. Suppose that
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{s\times n}
random sampling matrix such that

\Pr\left\{\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{X}\leq\alpha\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{Y}\right\}\geq 1-\delta_{1}

and for any fixed
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\Pr\left\{\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{Y}\leq\beta\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{X}\right\}\geq 1-\delta_{2}.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
satisfy

\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{Y}\leq\gamma\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{Y}.

Then, with probability at least
1-(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2})
,

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{X}\leq((\gamma+1)\alpha\beta+1)\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{X}

Proof.

We condition on the two guarantees of
\mathbf{S}
. Let

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{X}=\mathsf{OPT}=\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{X}.

We then have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{X}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{X}+\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{X}

triangle inequality

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{X}+\mathsf{OPT}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}\right\rVert_{Y}+\mathsf{OPT}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\left\lparen\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{Y}+\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{Y}\right\rparen+\mathsf{OPT}

triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq(\gamma+1)\alpha\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{S}\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{Y}+\mathsf{OPT}

optimality

\displaystyle\leq(\gamma+1)\alpha\beta\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}^{*}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{X}+\mathsf{OPT}

\displaystyle\leq((\gamma+1)\alpha\beta+1)\mathsf{OPT}.\qed

Note that this can easily be boosted to a
1-\delta
probability at a loss of a
\log(1/\delta)
factor in the query complexity by using a boosting procedure described in
[
MMWY22
]
.

14.2
Upper Bounds

By combining Lemma
14.1
with subspace embedding results, we immediately obtain many results for active regression. The first is a nearly optimal deterministic algorithm for
\ell_{\infty}
active regression, using Theorem
3.6
:

Theorem 14.2
.

There is a deterministic algorithm which, given
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, reads
O(d\log\log d)
entries of
\mathbf{b}
and outputs
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(\sqrt{d})\mathsf{OPT}.

A faster algorithm based running in input sparsity by sampling is also available, via Theorem
3.9
. This loses a
\log d
factor in the sample complexity. We can also generalize this result to the average top
k
loss, using our results in Section
3.3.1
.

For finite
p
, inspired by
[
WY22
]
, we obtain a whole set of trade-offs by using Theorem
2.4
to switch to the
\ell_{q}
norm and then using
\ell_{q}
Lewis weight sampling as in Theorem
2.3
to sample rows.

Theorem 14.3
.

Let
2\leq q<p<\infty
. There is an algorithm which, given
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, reads
O(d^{q/2}\log^{3}d)
entries of
\mathbf{b}
and outputs
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
such that

\Pr\left\{\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq O(d^{\frac{1}{2}\left\lparen 1-\frac{q}{p}\right\rparen})\mathsf{OPT}\right\}\geq\frac{99}{100}.

Proof.

We take
\mathbf{S}
to be the composition of the reweighting matrix
\mathbf{W}^{1/q-1/p}
where
\mathbf{w}
are the
\ell_{p}
Lewis weights of
\mathbf{A}
with an appropriate scaling, and the
\ell_{q}
Lewis weight sampling matrix which samples
O(d^{q/2}\log^{3}d)
rows of
\mathbf{W}^{1/q-1/p}\mathbf{A}
. By Theorems
2.4
and
2.3
,
\mathbf{S}
satisfies

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{q}\leq O(d^{\frac{1}{2}\left\lparen 1-\frac{q}{p}\right\rparen})\left\lVert\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{p}

with probability at least
1/200
. Furthermore, by Lemma
2.5
and Markov’s inequality, we have that
\left\lVert\mathbf{S}\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{q}\leq O(d^{\frac{1}{2}\left\lparen 1-\frac{q}{p}\right\rparen})\left\lVert\mathbf{y}\right\rVert_{p}
with probability at least
1/200
. Lemma
14.1
then gives the claimed result.
∎

14.3
Lower Bounds

We give two lower bounds, one for active regression in
\ell_{\infty}
and one for
\ell_{p}
with large distortion.

Theorem 14.4

(
\ell_{\infty}
Active Regression Lower Bound)
.

Let
C>1
be a constant. There is a constant
c
such that any randomized algorithm solves the
\ell_{\infty}
regression up to a relative error of
c\sqrt{d}
for some sufficiently small constant
c
and queries
m
entries in expectation and is correct with probability at least
0.99
. Then,
m=\Omega(d^{C})
.

Proof.

We use Theorem
13.1
to construct a set of
m=\Omega(d^{C})
vectors
S\subseteq\{\pm 1\}^{d}
such that for every distinct
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in S
, we have
\left\lvert\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq c^{-1}\sqrt{d}
for some
c<1
. Now let
\mathbf{A}
be the
m\times d
matrix with the vectors of
S
in its rows, and let
\mathbf{b}
be a zero vector with probability
1/2
and a uniformly random standard basis vector scaled by
d
with probability
1/2
. As reasoned in Theorem
13.2
, we may assume that our algorithm is deterministic and has success probability at least
0.99
over our hard random instance. Since
\mathbf{b}
is the zero vector with probability
1/2
, the algorithm must output
\mathbf{x}=0
if it reads
2m
zeros. However, for any
\mathbf{b}=d\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i}
for
i\in[n]
, the cost is
d
, whereas a cost of
c^{-1}\sqrt{d}
can be obtained if we choose
\mathbf{x}
to be the row of
\mathbf{A}
corresponding to this
i
. Thus, in order to obtain a distortion smaller than
c\sqrt{d}
, the algorithm must read at least
\Omega(m)
entries.
∎

Theorem 14.5

(
\ell_{p}
Active Regression Lower Bound)
.

Let
2\leq q<p
. There is a constant
c
such that any randomized algorithm solves the
\ell_{p}
regression up to a relative error of
cd^{\frac{1}{2}\left\lparen 1-\frac{q}{p}\right\rparen}
for some sufficiently small constant
c
and queries
m
entries in expectation and is correct with probability at least
0.99
. Then,
m=\Omega(d^{q/2})
.

Proof.

We use Theorem
13.1
to construct a set of
m=\Omega(d^{q/2})
vectors
S\subseteq\{\pm 1\}^{d}
such that for every distinct
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in S
, we have
\left\lvert\left\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq c^{-1}\sqrt{d}
for some
c<1
. Now let
\mathbf{A}
be the
m\times d
matrix with the vectors of
S
in its rows, and let
\mathbf{b}
be a zero vector with probability
1/2
and a uniformly random standard basis vector scaled by
d
with probability
1/2
. As reasoned in Theorem
13.2
, we may assume that our algorithm is deterministic and has success probability at least
0.99
over our hard random instance. Since
\mathbf{b}
is the zero vector with probability
1/2
, the algorithm must output
\mathbf{x}=0
if it reads
2m
zeros. However, for any
\mathbf{b}=d\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i}
for
i\in[n]
, the cost is
d
, whereas a cost of

m^{1/p}\cdot c^{-1}\sqrt{d}=c^{-1}d^{\frac{1}{2}\left\lparen\frac{q}{p}+1\right\rparen}

can be obtained if we choose
\mathbf{x}
to be the row of
\mathbf{A}
corresponding to this
i
. Thus, in order to obtain a distortion smaller than
c\sqrt{d}
, the algorithm must read at least
\Omega(m)
entries.
∎

Appendix A
Reduction from Existential to Algorithmic Column Subset Selection

We show an improvement and generalization of the argument of
[
MW21
]
, which shows that an existential result showing the existence of
s=s(k)
columns with a distortion of
\kappa(d)
on any
n\times d
instance for rank
k
approximation implies an algorithmic version which selects
O(s\log d)
columns with a distortion of
O(\kappa(2s+1))
. Note that the number of columns can only depend on
k
, whereas the distortion can depend on
d
.

Definition A.1
.

Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}_{*}+\boldsymbol{\Delta}
, where
\mathbf{A}_{*}
is the best rank
k
approximation in the entrywise
g
norm, that is,

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}\right\rVert_{g}=\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\right\rVert_{g}.

Let the columns of
\boldsymbol{\Delta}
be
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{1},\boldsymbol{\delta}^{2},\dots,\boldsymbol{\delta}^{d}
.

Definition A.2
.

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}
. Then:

•

Let
s(k)
denote the maximum size of a set of columns
S
for any
n\times d
instance
\mathbf{B}
for rank
k
approximation in the entrywise
g
-norm that can achieve a
\kappa(d)
approximation, that is, there exists a set
S\subseteq[d]
such that

\min_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times d}}\left\lVert\mathbf{B}-\mathbf{B}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{g}\leq\kappa(d)\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}\right\rVert_{g}

(21)

•

Let
T_{l}\subseteq[d]
denote the subset of columns surviving after the
l
th round of the algorithm. We assume without loss of generality that
T_{l}=[d_{l}]
for some
d_{l}\leq d
. Furthermore, we assume without loss of generality that
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{1}\rVert_{g}\geq\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{2}\rVert_{g}\geq\dots\geq\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{d_{l}}\rVert_{g}
.

•

Let
\mathsf{Res}_{l}\coloneqq\sum_{j=d_{l}/4}^{d_{l}}\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}
denote the residual cost, after restricting to the surviving columns and after removing the columns with cost in the top quarter.

Algorithm 7

Column Subset Selection for
M
-Estimators

input:
Input matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, rank
k
, loss function
g
, parameter
s
.

output:
Subset
T\subseteq[d]
of
O(s\log d)
columns.

1:

T_{0}\leftarrow[d]

2:

while

\lvert T_{l}\rvert\geq 1000s

do

3:

t_{l}\leftarrow 30s

4:

for

t=1,2,\dots,O(\log\log d)

do

5:

Sample
H\sim\binom{T_{l}}{t_{l}}

6:

Let
\mathbf{x}^{j}
minimize
\min_{\mathbf{x}}\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{g}
up to a
\mathsf{reg}_{g,t_{l}}
factor for each
j\in T_{l}

7:

Let
F_{l,t}
be the
d_{l}/20=\lvert T_{l}\rvert/20
columns with smallest regression cost
\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}^{j}-\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{g}

8:

C_{l,t}\leftarrow\sum_{j\in F_{l,t}}\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}^{j}-\mathbf{a}^{j}\rVert_{g}

9:

Let
t^{*}
be the
t
with smallest
C_{l,t}

10:

T_{l+1}\leftarrow T_{l}\setminus F_{l,t^{*}}

Theorem A.3

(Generalization and Improvement of
[
MW21
]
)
.

Consider the definitions in Definition
A.2
. Suppose that there is an algorithm outputting
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{B}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{g}\leq\mathsf{reg}_{g,s}\cdot\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{s}}\left\lVert\mathbf{B}\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{g}

for any
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times s}
and
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, Algorithm
7
outputs a subset
S\subseteq[d]
of
\lvert S\rvert=O(s\log d)
columns and
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{S\times d}
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{A}-\mathbf{A}|^{S}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{g}

\displaystyle\leq O(\kappa)\mathsf{reg}_{g,O(s)}\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\lVert\mathbf{A}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\rVert_{g}

We present the following main lemma, which follows
[
MW21
, Claim 2.6]
but also makes some additional improvements to remove a log factor:

Lemma A.4
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
s=s(k)
and
\kappa=\kappa(2s+1)
. Let
H\sim\binom{[d]}{2s}
and let
i\sim[d]\setminus H
. Then,

\Pr\left\{\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{H}}\left\lVert\mathbf{a}^{i}-\mathbf{A}|^{H}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{g}\leq\frac{600\kappa}{d_{l}}\mathsf{Res}_{l}\right\}\geq\frac{1}{10}

Proof.

Let
G\coloneqq[d_{l}]\setminus[d_{l}/4]
. Note that
\E\lvert G\cap H\rvert\geq 20s
. By Chernoff bounds, with probability at least
99/100
, we have that
\left\lvert G\cap H\right\rvert\geq 4s
. We conditioned on this event.

Let
H^{\prime}
be a uniformly random subset of
G\cap H
of size
2s
. Let
R=R(H^{\prime}\cup\{i\})
be the set of
s(k)
columns satisfying (
21
). Then by Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\Pr_{H^{\prime}}\left\{\sum_{j\in H^{\prime}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}\geq 20\frac{s}{\lvert G\rvert}\sum_{j\in G}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}\right\}\leq\frac{\E_{H^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{j\in H^{\prime}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}\right]}{20\frac{s}{\lvert G\rvert}\sum_{j\in G}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}}\leq\frac{1}{10}

and similarly,

\Pr_{i}\left\{\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{i}\right\rVert_{g}\geq\frac{10}{\lvert G\rvert}\sum_{j\in G}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}\right\}\leq\frac{\E_{i}\left[\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{i}\right\rVert_{g}\right]}{\frac{5}{\lvert G\rvert}\sum_{j\in G}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}}\leq\frac{1}{10}

Now note that conditioned on the choice of
H^{\prime}\cup\{i\}
,
i
is a uniformly random element of
H^{\prime}\cup\{i\}
, so
\Pr\{i\notin R\}\geq 1/2
. Furthermore,

\min_{\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times(2s+1)}}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H^{\prime}\cup\{i\}}-\mathbf{A}|^{R}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{g}\leq\kappa\min_{\rank(\hat{\mathbf{A}})\leq k}\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{H^{\prime}\cup\{i\}}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}\right\rVert_{g}\leq\kappa\cdot\left\lVert\Delta|^{H^{\prime}\cup\{i\}}\right\rVert_{g}

so by Markov’s inequality,

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{R}}\left\lVert\mathbf{a}^{i}-\mathbf{A}|^{R}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert\leq\frac{10\kappa}{s}\left\lVert\Delta|^{H^{\prime}\cup\{i\}}\right\rVert_{g}

with probability at least
9/10
. By a union bound, we have that with probability at least

1-\frac{1}{100}-\frac{1}{10}-\frac{1}{10}-\frac{1}{10}\geq\frac{1}{10},

we have

\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{R}}\left\lVert\mathbf{a}^{i}-\mathbf{A}|^{R}\mathbf{x}\right\rVert_{g}\leq\frac{10\kappa}{s}\left\lparen\frac{10}{\lvert G\rvert}\sum_{j\in G}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}+20\frac{s}{\lvert G\rvert}\sum_{j\in G}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}\right\rparen\leq\frac{400\kappa}{\lvert G\rvert}\sum_{j\in G}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\right\rVert_{g}.

To conclude, note that
\lvert G\rvert=d_{l}-d_{l}/4=3d_{l}/4
and that we can pad
\mathbf{x}
with zeros on coordinates in
H\setminus R
.
∎

We then just mimic the proof of Theorem
1.5
to complete the proof.

Proof of Theorem
A.3
.

Note first that the algorithm decreases the size of
T_{l}
by a
(1-1/20)
factor at each iteration. Thus, the algorithm makes at most
L=O(\log d)
iterations of the outer loop. By averaging Lemma
A.4
over the
3d_{l}/4
bottom columns, we have a probability of at least
1/20
of choosing
d_{l}/20
columns such that the total cost is at most

O(\kappa)\cdot\mathsf{Res}_{l}.

Since we repeat
O(\log L)=O(\log\log d)
times and use an
\mathsf{reg}_{g,t_{l}}
-approximate regression algorithm, we with probability at least
1-1/100L
, we find
d_{l}/20
columns
F_{l}\subseteq T_{l}
and corresponding coefficients
\mathbf{X}
such that

\left\lVert\mathbf{A}|^{F_{l}}-\mathbf{A}|^{S_{l}}\mathbf{X}\right\rVert_{g}\leq O(\kappa)\mathsf{reg}_{g,t_{l}}\mathsf{Res}_{l}.

Thus, our total cost is

\sum_{l=1}^{O(\log d)}O(\kappa)\mathsf{reg}_{g,t_{l}}\mathsf{Res}_{l}.

Finally, as argued in
[
SWZ19b
,
MW21
]
, we show that
\sum_{l}\mathsf{Res}_{l}=O(\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}\rVert_{g})
. Note that if a column
j
contributes to
\mathsf{Res}_{l}
, then it must be in the bottom
3/4
fraction of the
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}
in round
l
. Then since the bottom
1/20
fraction of
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}
is fitted and removed in each round,
\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}
can only contribute to
\mathsf{Res}_{l}
in
O(1)
rounds. Thus, the sum is bounded by
O(1)\sum_{j}\lVert\boldsymbol{\delta}^{j}\rVert_{g}=O(\lVert\boldsymbol{\Delta}\rVert_{g})
.

The total number of columns selected is
O(s)
in each of the
O(\log d)
rounds, for a total of
O(s\log d)
.
∎

Appendix B
Feldman–Langberg Framework with Independent Sampling

We adapt arguments in
[
FL11
,
BFL16
,
FSS20
]
. Recall the following definitions from the preliminaries section of
[
FSS20
]
.

Definition B.1

(Range space)
.

A
range space
is a pair
\mathfrak{R}=(F,\ranges)
where
F
is a set called a
ground set
and
\ranges
is a family of subsets of
F
.

Definition B.2

(VC-dimension)
.

The
VC-dimension
of a range space
\mathfrak{R}=(F,\ranges)
is the size
\left\lvert G\right\rvert
of the largest subset
G\subseteq F
such that

\left\lvert\left\{G\cap\range\right\}:\range\in\ranges\right\rvert=2^{\left\lvert G\right\rvert}

Definition B.3

(
(\eta,\varepsilon)
-approximation)
.

Let
\eta,\varepsilon>0
and
\mathfrak{R}=(F,\ranges)
be a range space with finite
F\neq\varnothing
. An
(\eta,\varepsilon)
-approximation of
\mathfrak{R}
is a set
S\subseteq F
such that for all
\range\in\ranges
, we have

\left\lvert\frac{\left\lvert\range\cap F\right\rvert}{\left\lvert F\right\rvert}-\frac{\left\lvert\range\cap S\right\rvert}{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}\right\rvert\leq\begin{cases}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{\left\lvert\range\cap F\right\rvert}{\left\lvert F\right\rvert}&\text{if $\left\lvert\range\cap F\right\rvert\geq\eta\left\lvert F\right\rvert$}\\
\varepsilon\cdot\eta&\text{if $\left\lvert\range\cap F\right\rvert<\eta\left\lvert F\right\rvert$}\\
\end{cases}

Definition B.4
.

Let
F
be a finite set of functions from a set
\mathcal{Q}
to
[0,\infty)
. For every
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
and
r\geq 0
, let

\displaystyle\range(F,Q,r)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{f\in F:f(Q)\geq r\right\}

\displaystyle\ranges(F)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\range(F,Q,r):Q\in\mathcal{Q},r\geq 0\right\}

Then,
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathcal{Q},F}\coloneqq(F,\ranges(F))
is the
range space
induced by
\mathcal{Q}
and
F
.

We extract the following lemma from
[
FSS20
, Theorem 31]
, which is based on works of
[
FL11
,
BFL16
]
:s

Theorem B.5

(Range Space Approximation to Coresets, Theorem 31,
[
FSS20
]
)
.

Let
F
be a finite set of
n=\left\lvert F\right\rvert
functions from a set
\mathcal{Q}
to
[0,\infty)
, and let
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
. Let

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}\geq\sup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\frac{f(Q)}{\sum_{h\in F}h(Q)},\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}=\sum_{f\in F}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}.

Furthermore, assume that for each
f\in F
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}\geq 1/n
and that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}
is an integer power of
2
, so that for some
n^{*}\in\mathbb{N}
,
n^{*}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}\in\mathbb{N}
for all
f\in F
. Let
F^{\prime}
be obtained by replacing each
f\in F
by
n_{f}\coloneqq n^{*}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}
copies of
f/n_{f}
. Suppose
S\subseteq F^{\prime}
is an
(\eta,\varepsilon/2)
-approximation for
\eta=1/\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
to the range space
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathcal{Q},F^{\prime}}
. Then, for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}

\left\lvert\frac{\left\lvert F^{\prime}\right\rvert}{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}\sum_{f\in S}f(Q)-\sum_{f\in F}f(Q)\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon\sum_{h\in F}h(Q)

The proof of
[
FSS20
, Theorem 31]
uses the following result to obtain an
(\eta,\varepsilon)
-approximation of
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathcal{Q},F^{\prime}}
:

Theorem B.6

(Theorem 5,
[
LLS01
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{R}=(F,\ranges)
with finite
F\neq\varnothing
be a range space with VC-dimension
d
,
\eta>0
, and
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. There is a universal constant
c>0
such that a sample of

s\geq\frac{c}{\eta\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\left\lparen d\log\frac{1}{\eta}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen

elements drawn independently and uniformly at random from
F
is an
(\eta,\varepsilon)
-approximation for
(F,\ranges)
with probability at least
1-\delta
.

We first note that the uniform sampling with replacement can be replaced by uniform sampling without replacement, at a sacrifice of
s^{2}/n
in the success probability.

Corollary B.7

(
[
LLS01
]
by Sampling without Replacement)
.

Let
\mathfrak{R}=(F,\ranges)
with finite
F\neq\varnothing
be a range space with VC-dimension
d
,
\eta>0
, and
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. There is a universal constant
c>0
such that a sample of

s\geq\frac{c}{\eta\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\left\lparen d\log\frac{1}{\eta}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen

distinct
elements drawn uniformly at random from
F
is an
(\eta,\varepsilon)
-approximation for
(F,\ranges)
with probability at least
1-\delta-s^{2}/n
.

Proof.

The probability that a uniform sample with replacement selects an item twice is less than
s^{2}/n
by the union bound. Then with probability at least
1-s^{2}/n
, the uniform sample with replacement selects
s
unique elements. Conditioned on this event, by symmetry, the sample is drawn as a uniformly random subset of size
s
. Now suppose that the conclusion is false. Let good denote the event that uniform sampling with replacement is successful, and let unique denote the event that uniform sampling with replacement selects a unique set of
s
elements. Then,

\displaystyle\Pr(\text{good})

\displaystyle=\Pr(\text{good}\mid\text{unique})\Pr(\text{unique})+\Pr(\text{good}\mid\neg\text{unique})\Pr(\neg\text{unique})

\displaystyle<\Pr(\text{good}\mid\text{unique})+\frac{s^{2}}{n}

\displaystyle<1-\delta-\frac{s^{2}}{n}+\frac{s^{2}}{n}

\displaystyle=1-\delta,

which contradicts the conclusion of Theorem
B.6
.
∎

We now switch to sampling each of the
n^{*}\cdot\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
items independently with probability
(1+\varepsilon)s/n
. Note then that with probability at least
1-\delta
, we sample at least
s
and at most
(1+\varepsilon)^{2}s
elements by Chernoff bounds, since
s\geq c\varepsilon^{-2}\log\frac{1}{\delta}
. Furthermore, conditioned on the number
\left\lvert S\right\rvert
of elements sampled, the sample
S
is a uniformly random subset of size
\left\lvert S\right\rvert
by symmetry. Furthermore, conditioned on each of these, we obtain a good approximation with probability at least
1-\delta-\left\lvert S\right\rvert^{2}/n
by Corollary
B.7
. This yields the following:

Corollary B.8

(
[
LLS01
]
by Independent Sampling)
.

Let
\mathfrak{R}=(F,\ranges)
with finite
F\neq\varnothing
be a range space with VC-dimension
d
,
\eta>0
, and
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. There is a universal constant
c>0
such that if

s\geq\frac{c}{\eta\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\left\lparen d\log\frac{1}{\eta}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen,

then a sample
S
obtained by independently sampling each
f\in F
with probability
s/n
is an
(\eta,\varepsilon)
-approximation for
\mathfrak{R}
with probability at least
1-\delta-4s^{2}/n
.

Now by combining Corollary
B.8
with Theorem
B.5
yields the following:

Theorem B.9

(
[
FL11
,
BFL16
,
FSS20
]
by Independent Sampling)
.

Let
F
be a finite set of
n=\left\lvert F\right\rvert
functions from a set
\mathcal{Q}
to
[0,\infty)
, and let
\delta,\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
. Let

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}\geq\sup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\frac{f(Q)}{\sum_{h\in F}h(Q)},\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}=\sum_{f\in F}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}.

Let
\mathcal{S}\geq\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
be an upper bound on the total sensitivity
\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
.
Furthermore, assume that for each
f\in F
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}\geq 1/n
and that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}
is an integer power of
2
, so that for some
n^{*}\in\mathbb{N}
,
n^{*}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}\in\mathbb{N}
for all
f\in F
. Choose this
n^{*}
so that
n^{*}\geq c^{\prime}\cdot n^{2}/\delta
for a sufficiently large constant
c^{\prime}
.

Let
F^{\prime}
be obtained by replacing each
f\in F
by
n_{f}\coloneqq n^{*}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}
copies of
f/n_{f}
. Let
d
be the VC-dimension of
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathcal{Q},F^{\prime}}
. Let

s\geq c\cdot\frac{\mathcal{S}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left\lparen d\log\mathcal{S}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen

for a sufficiently large constant
c
. Let
S
be a random sample obtained by sampling each
f\in F^{\prime}
with probability
s/(n^{*}\cdot\mathcal{S})
. Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, simultaneously for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}

\left\lvert\frac{\left\lvert F^{\prime}\right\rvert}{s}\sum_{f\in S}f(Q)-\sum_{f\in F}f(Q)\right\rvert\leq O(\varepsilon)\sum_{h\in F}h(Q)

Proof.

Note first that we can handle the fact that we only have an upper bound
\mathcal{S}
instead of
\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
by adding an empty function at the end of
F
with a sensitivity overestimate
\mathcal{S}-\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
, so the previous results assuming the knowledge of an exact sum
\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
apply. Second, note that by Chernoff bounds,
\left\lvert S\right\rvert=(1\pm\varepsilon)s
. Thus,
\left\lvert F^{\prime}\right\rvert/\left\lvert S\right\rvert=(1\pm\varepsilon)\left\lvert F^{\prime}\right\rvert/s
which yields the conclusion. Finally, the success probability is
1-O(\delta)
since we either select
n
elements, in which case we deterministically get an exact approximation, or
s^{2}/n^{*}\leq\delta
, in which case the failure probability is as claimed by Corollary
B.8
.
∎

Remark B.10
.

Note that splitting
f
into
n_{f}=n^{*}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{f}
copies of
f/n_{f}
and sampling each with probability
p_{f}=s/(n^{*}\cdot\mathcal{S})
can be combined into just sampling a weight
w_{f}\sim\Binomial(n_{f},p_{f})
.

B.1
Improved Feldman–Langberg Framework for Clustering

Next, we adapt the improved
[
FL11
]
argument for clustering which achieves a linear dependence on
k
, from Theorem 15.5. We first set up some notation:

Definition B.11
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, let
p\geq 1
, and let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a set of centers (not necessarily of size
k
) such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},B)^{p}\leq\gamma\cdot\min_{C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d},\left\lvert C\right\rvert\leq k}\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}

for some
\gamma>0
. Let
\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq\argmin_{\mathbf{b}\in B}\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{i}-\mathbf{b}\right\rVert_{2}
be the center in
B
closest to
\mathbf{a}_{i}
, and let
P_{i}\subseteq[n]
denote the set of indices belonging to the set cluster as
i\in[n]
.

We sample each row with probability

p_{i}\geq\min\left\{1,\max\left\{\beta_{1}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p+1}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{i}-\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},B)^{p}},\beta_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\frac{1}{\left\lvert P_{i}\right\rvert}\right\}\right\}

(22)

and set the weight to be
\mathbf{w}_{i}=1/p_{i}
if we sample the row and
0
otherwise, for oversampling parameters
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\geq 1
.

First, we show that the above sampling algorithm preserves the sizes of the clusters.

Lemma B.12

(Preserving Size of Clusters)
.

Let
P\subseteq[n]
be all the rows clustered to a given center in
B
. Then,

\sum_{i\in P}\mathbf{w}_{i}=(1\pm\varepsilon)\left\lvert P\right\rvert

with probability at least
1-2\exp(-\beta_{2}/3)
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbf{w}_{i}\leq\varepsilon^{2}\left\lvert P\right\rvert/\beta_{2}
for all
i\in P
, so
\beta_{2}\mathbf{w}_{i}/(\varepsilon^{2}\left\lvert P\right\rvert)\in[0,1]
, and

\E\left[\sum_{i\in P}\mathbf{w}_{i}\right]=\left\lvert P\right\rvert.

Then by Chernoff bounds,

\Pr\left\{\left\lvert\sum_{i\in P}\mathbf{w}_{i}-\left\lvert P\right\rvert\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon\left\lvert P\right\rvert\right\}\leq 2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon^{2}\beta_{2}\frac{\left\lvert P\right\rvert}{\left\lvert P\right\rvert}\right\rparen\leq 2\exp(-\beta_{2}/3).\qed

By taking
\beta_{2}\geq 3\log(\left\lvert B\right\rvert/\delta)
, the above holds for all centers
B
with probability at least
1-2\delta
. We condition on this event for the rest of this section.

We will need the following relaxed triangle inequality:

Lemma B.13

(Relaxed Triangle Inequality, Corollary A.2,
[
MMR19
]
)
.

For vectors
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}
and
\varepsilon>0
and
p\geq 1
, we have

\left\lVert\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\left\lVert\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{v}\right\rVert+\left\lparen\frac{1+\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right\rparen^{p-1}\left\lVert\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{w}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}.

Next, we give a net argument to show that it suffices to preserve the cost of centers
C
in a set of size at most
\poly(n)^{dk}
.

Lemma B.14

(Net argument)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1/4)
with
\varepsilon\geq 1/\poly(n)
. Let
\mathbf{w}_{i}
be sampled as in (
22
) with
\beta_{2}\geq 3\log(\left\lvert B\right\rvert/\delta)
. With probability at least
1-\delta
, here is a set
\mathcal{N}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}
with
\left\lvert\mathcal{N}\right\rvert\leq\poly(n)^{dk}
such that if

\mbox{for all $C\in\mathcal{N}$},\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}=(1\pm\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}

(23)

then we have that

\mbox{for all $C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d},\left\lvert C\right\rvert\leq k$},\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}=(1\pm 10\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}.

Proof.

Note that all the
\mathbf{a}_{i}
are within a distance of
\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}^{1/p}
of
B
, and a distance of
2\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}^{1/p}
of every point in the same cluster
P_{i}
. Now suppose that
\mathbf{a}_{i}
is assigned to some center in a cluster
C
with
d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}\geq 3^{p}\gamma\mathsf{OPT}/\varepsilon^{p}
. Then, for any
j\in P_{i}
,

d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)\geq d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)-\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{i}-\mathbf{a}_{j}\right\rVert_{2}\geq 2\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}^{1/p}/\varepsilon

so

\displaystyle d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)

\displaystyle=d(\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\prime},C)\pm\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\prime}-\mathbf{a}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=d(\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\prime},C)\pm 2\gamma^{1/p}\mathsf{OPT}^{1/p}

\displaystyle=d(\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\prime},C)\pm\varepsilon d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)

so
d(\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\prime},C)=(1\pm\varepsilon)d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)
. Then by Lemma
B.12
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in P_{i}}\mathbf{w}_{j}d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)^{p}=(1\pm\varepsilon)\sum_{j\in P_{i}}\mathbf{w}_{j}d(\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\prime},C)^{p}=(1\pm\varepsilon)^{2}\sum_{j\in P_{i}}d(\mathbf{a}_{j}^{\prime},C)^{p}=(1\pm\varepsilon)^{3}\sum_{j\in P_{i}}d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)^{p}.

Thus, it suffices to consider centers with
d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}\leq\alpha\mathsf{OPT}\coloneqq 3^{p}\gamma\mathsf{OPT}/\varepsilon^{p}
.

Let
C^{*}
be an optimal solution achieving
\mathsf{OPT}
, and consider a ball
\mathcal{B}_{l}
of radius
(3\gamma^{1/p}/\varepsilon+1)\mathsf{OPT}^{1/p}
centered at each of the
k
centers
l\in[k]
. Note that every point
\mathbf{a}_{i}
is within a distance of
\mathsf{OPT}^{1/p}
of one of the centers, so if a
k
-tuple of centers
C
has any point outside of these
k
balls, then by the triangle inequality, that point alone already has a cost of at least
\alpha\mathsf{OPT}
and thus we need not consider it.

We now consider an
\varepsilon^{\prime}
-net
\mathcal{N}_{l}
for each
\mathcal{B}_{l}
, for
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\mathsf{OPT}^{1/p}/\poly(n)
. Note that each net has size at most
\poly(\alpha n)^{d}
. We then set
\mathcal{N}
to be the
k
tuples of the union of these
k
nets.

Now let
C
be any set of
k
centers satisfying within a cost of
\alpha\mathsf{OPT}
. Then, as argued previously, each point lies in one of the
\mathcal{B}_{l}
, so we can choose a
C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
such that for each point in
C
, there is a point in
C^{\prime}
at a distance of at most
\mathsf{OPT}^{1/p}/\poly(n)
. Then by the relaxed triangle inequality,

d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}=(1\pm\varepsilon)d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C^{\prime})^{p}\pm\left\lparen\frac{1+\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\right\rparen^{p-1}\frac{\mathsf{OPT}}{\poly(n)}=(1\pm\varepsilon)d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C^{\prime})^{p}\pm\frac{\varepsilon}{\poly(n)}\mathsf{OPT}

so summing over
i\in[n]
gives

\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}=(1\pm\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C^{\prime})^{p}\pm\varepsilon\mathsf{OPT}

and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}=(1\pm\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C^{\prime})^{p}\pm\varepsilon\mathsf{OPT}

since
\mathbf{w}_{i}
is bounded by
\poly(n)
. Note also that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C^{\prime})^{p}\geq(1-\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C^{\prime})^{p}\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathsf{OPT}

so

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C^{\prime})^{p}-\varepsilon\mathsf{OPT}\geq(1-2\varepsilon)\mathsf{OPT}

Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}

\displaystyle=(1\pm\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C^{\prime})^{p}\pm\varepsilon\mathsf{OPT}

\displaystyle=(1\pm 2\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C^{\prime})^{p}

\displaystyle=(1\pm 2\varepsilon)(1\pm\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C^{\prime})^{p}

\displaystyle=(1\pm 2\varepsilon)(1\pm\varepsilon)\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}\pm\varepsilon\mathsf{OPT}\right]

\displaystyle=(1\pm 2\varepsilon)(1\pm\varepsilon)\left\lparen 1\pm\frac{\varepsilon}{1-2\varepsilon}\right\rparen\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}

\displaystyle=(1\pm 10\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}

for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small.
∎

With the net argument in hand, we seek to prove (
23
). We will need the following lemma of
[
HV20
]
, which fixes a claim in
[
FL11
, Theorem 15.5]
.

Lemma B.15

(Lemma 8.1,
[
HV20
]
)
.

If

\left\lvert d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}-d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}\right\rvert\geq\frac{p\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{i}-\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}}{\varepsilon^{p-1}},

then

\left\lvert d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}-d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}\right\rvert\leq p\varepsilon\cdot\max\left\{d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p},d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}\right\}

so
d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)=(1\pm\varepsilon)d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)
.

Now, the idea of
[
FL11
, Theorem 15.5]
is that since it is easy to preserve the costs
d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)
using, for example, Lemma
B.12
, it now suffices to preserve the
difference
between
d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}
and
d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)
. This will either be bounded by
\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{i}-\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}/\varepsilon^{p-1}
or be larger than it; in the former case, we have a good bound for use in a Bernstein bound, while in the latter,
d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}
must be close to
d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}
by Lemma
B.15
.

Lemma B.16

(Bernstein Bounds)
.

Fix
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\lvert C\right\rvert\leq k
. Let
\mathbf{w}_{i}
be sampled as in (
22
). Then, with probability at least
1-2\exp(-\beta_{1}/2^{p+2})-2\left\lvert B\right\rvert\exp(-\beta_{2})
, we have that

\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}\right\rvert\leq O(\gamma\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}

Proof.

We partition the rows
[n]
into two parts,
G
and
B=[n]\setminus G
, where

G=\left\{i\in[n]:\left\lvert d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}-d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}\right\rvert\leq\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{i}-\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}}{\varepsilon^{p-1}}\right\}.

For indices in
G
, we use Bernstein bounds to bound

\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)(d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}-d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p})\right\rvert

For
i\in G
, we have that

\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lvert d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}-d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}\right\rvert\leq\mathbf{w}_{i}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{i}-\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}}{\varepsilon^{p-1}}\leq\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{j},B)^{p}\leq\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}\gamma\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)^{p}

and thus the variance is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in G}p_{i}\cdot\left\lparen\mathbf{w}_{i}\left\lvert d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}-d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}\right\rvert\right\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}\varepsilon^{2}\left\lparen\sum_{i\in G}\left\lvert d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}-d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}\right\rvert\right\rparen\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{j},B)^{p}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}\varepsilon^{2}\left\lparen\sum_{i\in G}(2^{p-1}+1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}+2^{p-1}\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{i}-\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}\right\rparen\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{j},B)^{p}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{p}}{\beta_{1}}\varepsilon^{2}\gamma^{2}\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)^{p}\right\rparen^{2}

Then Bernstein bounds give that

\displaystyle\Pr\left\{\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)(d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}-d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p})\right\rvert\geq\gamma\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle 2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}\varepsilon^{2}\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}\right\rparen^{2}}{2^{p}\varepsilon^{2}\gamma\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)^{p}\right\rparen^{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\gamma^{2}\left\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)^{p}\right\rparen^{2}/3}\beta_{1}\right\rparen\leq 2\exp\left\lparen-\frac{\beta_{1}}{2^{p+2}}\right\rparen.

Now condition on the complement of this event, as well as the event of Lemma
B.12
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}-(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}+(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}-(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}\right\rvert+\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)(d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}-d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p})\right\rvert+\left\lvert\sum_{i\in G}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}+\varepsilon\sum_{i\in G}d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}+2^{p-1}\varepsilon\sum_{i\in G}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}+\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{i}-\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq(\gamma+(\gamma+1)2^{p-1})\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}

\displaystyle\leq(2^{p}+1)\gamma\varepsilon\sum_{j=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}.

For indices in
B
, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in B}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}

\displaystyle=(1\pm\varepsilon)\sum_{i\in B}\mathbf{w}_{i}d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}

Lemma
B.15

\displaystyle=(1\pm\varepsilon)^{2}\sum_{i\in B}d(\mathbf{a}_{i}^{\prime},C)^{p}

Lemma
B.12

\displaystyle=(1\pm\varepsilon)^{3}\sum_{i\in B}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}

\displaystyle\text{Lemma \ref{lem:hv2020-lem8}}.

Altogether, we conclude that

\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}\right\rvert\leq O(\gamma\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}.\qed

Finally, we are ready to put all the pieces together.

Theorem B.17
.

Let
\mathbf{w}_{i}
be drawn as in (
22
), with

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen dk\log n+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\beta_{2}

\displaystyle=O\left\lparen\log\left\lvert B\right\rvert+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen

Then, for all
C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\lvert C\right\rvert\leq k
, we have that

\left\lvert\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-1)d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}\right\rvert\leq O(\gamma\varepsilon)\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}.

Proof.

For the given choice of
\beta
, by Lemma
B.16
with, we can union bound over a net of size
\poly(n)^{dk}
from Lemma
B.14
, with probability
1-\delta
. The guarantee of the net argument then gives the lemma.
∎

In particular, we achieve a coreset of size

O\left\lparen\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p+1}}\left\lparen dk+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right\rparen+\frac{\left\lvert B\right\rvert}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log\left\lvert B\right\rvert\right\rparen

by summing over
i\in[n]
in (
22
). For constant
\delta
,
\left\lvert B\right\rvert=O(k)
, and
d=\log(k/\varepsilon)/\varepsilon^{2}
using a terminal embedding after a first
\poly(k/\varepsilon)
-sized coreset, this is size

\tilde{O}\left\lparen\frac{k}{\varepsilon^{p+3}}\right\rparen.

Appendix C
Online Euclidean
(k,p)
-Clustering

We show that online
k
-means clustering algorithm of
[
LSS16
]
immediately generalizes to Euclidean
(k,p)
clustering.

See
10.2

The algorithm and analysis of
[
LSS16
]
for the case of
p=2
generalizes with little obstructions. We work out the details below.

Algorithm 8

Online Euclidean
(k,p)
-Clustering
[
LSS16
]

input:

\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, number of clusters
k
, cost lower bound
w^{*}
.

output:
Online clusters.

1:

C\leftarrow\varnothing

2:

r\leftarrow 1
;
q_{1}\leftarrow 0
;
f_{1}\leftarrow w^{*}/k\log n

3:

for

i\in[n]

do

4:

p_{i}\leftarrow\min(1,d(\mathbf{a}_{i},C)^{p}/f_{r})

5:

C\leftarrow C\cup\{\mathbf{a}_{i}\}
and
q_{r}\leftarrow q_{r}+1
with probability
p_{i}

6:

if

q_{r}\geq 3k(1+\log_{2}n)

then

7:

r\leftarrow r+1
;
q_{r}\leftarrow 0
;
f_{r}\leftarrow 2\cdot f_{r-1}

8:

Assign
\mathbf{a}_{i}
to the closest center in
C

Let
S_{1}^{*},\dots,S_{k}^{*}\subseteq[n]
be an optimal partition with centers
\mathbf{c}_{1}^{*},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{k}^{*}
, and let

W_{i}^{*}=\sum_{j\in S_{i}^{*}}\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mathbf{c}_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{p},\qquad W^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}W_{i}^{*}

be the costs of the
i
th cluster and all the clusters, respectively. Let
A_{i}^{*}=W_{i}^{*}/\left\lvert S_{i}^{*}\right\rvert
be the average cost of a vector in the
i
th cluster. Also define rings

\displaystyle S_{i,0}^{*}

\displaystyle=\left\{j\in S_{i}^{*}:\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mathbf{c}_{i}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}\leq A_{i}^{*}\right\}

\displaystyle S_{i,\tau}^{*}

\displaystyle=\left\{j\in S_{i}^{*}:\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mathbf{c}_{i}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}\in\left(2^{\tau-1}A_{i}^{*},2^{\tau}A_{i}^{*}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle 1\leq\tau\leq\log_{2}n

Lemma C.1

(Bound on Number of Clusters)
.

The expected number of clusters formed by Algorithm
8
is at most

\E[\left\lvert C\right\rvert]=O\left\lparen k(\log n)\log\frac{W^{*}}{w^{*}}\right\rparen

Proof.

The proof is nearly identical to Theorem 1 of
[
LSS16
]
. Let
r^{\prime}
be the first round
r
such that

f_{r^{\prime}}\geq\frac{W^{*}}{k\log_{2}n}

There are at most
\log\frac{f_{r^{\prime}}}{f_{1}}
rounds before
r^{\prime}
, so the number of clusters opened before round
r^{\prime}
is at most
O\left\lparen k(\log n)\log\frac{W^{*}}{w^{*}}\right\rparen
. It suffices to bound the number of clusters opened after round
r^{\prime}
.

Fix a ring
\tau
, and define
S_{i,\tau,r}^{*}
to be the points in
S_{i,\tau}^{*}
encountered in round
r
. The first point
\ell
from
S_{i,\tau}^{*}
chosen to be a center contributes
1
towards the number of clusters opened, while the rest open

\sum_{r\geq r^{\prime}}\frac{2^{p}\cdot 2^{\tau}A_{i}^{*}}{f_{r}}\left\lvert S_{i,\tau,r}^{*}\right\rvert

in expectation, since the probability
p_{j}
of opening a new cluster in Line
4
is bounded by

\frac{d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)^{p}}{f_{r}}\leq\frac{d(\mathbf{a}_{j},\mathbf{a}_{\ell})^{p}}{f_{r}}\leq\frac{2^{p-1}\cdot(\left\lVert\mathbf{a}_{j}-\mathbf{c}_{i}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}^{p}+\left\lVert\mathbf{c}_{i}^{*}-\mathbf{a}_{\ell}\right\rVert_{2}^{p})}{f_{r}}\leq\frac{2^{p-1}\cdot 2\cdot 2^{\tau}A_{i}^{*}}{f_{r}}=\frac{2^{p}2^{\tau}A_{i}^{*}}{f_{r}}.

Then, summing over
\tau\geq 0
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\geq 0}\left\lparen 1+\sum_{r\geq r^{\prime}}\frac{2^{p}\cdot 2^{\tau}A_{i}^{*}}{f_{r}}\left\lvert S_{i,\tau,r}^{*}\right\rvert\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq O(\log n)+\sum_{\tau\geq 0}\sum_{r\geq r^{\prime}}\frac{2^{p}\cdot 2^{\tau}A_{i}^{*}}{f_{r^{\prime}}}\left\lvert S_{i,\tau,r}^{*}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 1+\log_{2}n+\frac{2^{p+1}}{f_{r^{\prime}}}\sum_{\tau\geq 0}2^{\tau-1}A_{i}^{*}\left\lvert S_{i,\tau}^{*}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 1+\log_{2}n+\frac{2^{p+1}}{f_{r^{\prime}}}\left\lparen A_{i}^{*}\left\lvert S_{i}^{*}\right\rvert+\sum_{\tau\geq 1}2^{\tau-1}A_{i}^{*}\left\lvert S_{i,\tau}^{*}\right\rvert\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq 1+\log_{2}n+\frac{2^{p+1}}{f_{r^{\prime}}}\left\lparen W_{i}^{*}+W_{i}^{*}\right\rparen

\displaystyle\leq 1+\log_{2}n+2^{p+2}k(\log_{2}n)\frac{W_{i}^{*}}{W^{*}}.

Then, summing over
i\in[k]
and using that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}W_{i}^{*}=W^{*}
then gives a bound of
(2^{p+2}+1)k(1+\log_{2}n)
.
∎

Lemma C.2

(Lemma 1,
[
LSS16
]
)
.

Let
p_{i}\geq\min\{A_{i}/B,1\}
be probabilities, for
A_{i}\geq 0
and
B\geq 0
. Let
t
be the number of sequential unsuccessful experiments. Then,

\E\left[\sum_{i=1}^{t}A_{i}\right]\leq B.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
R
denote the random variable representing the total number of rounds. Then,

\E[f_{R}]=O\left\lparen\frac{W^{*}}{k\log n}\right\rparen

Proof.

We now estimate
\E[f_{R}]
. Let
r^{\prime\prime}
be the first phase
r
such that

f_{r^{\prime\prime}}\geq\frac{2^{p+4}W^{*}}{k(1+\log_{2}n)}.

Following the proof of Lemma
C.1
, at most

k(1+\log_{2}n)+\frac{2^{p+2}}{f_{r^{\prime\prime}}}\leq\frac{5}{4}k(1+\log_{2}n)

clusters are opened after round
r^{\prime}
in expectation. By Markov’s inequality, the probability of opening more than
3k(1+\log_{2}n)
clusters is at most
4/9
. Then with probability at least
5/9
, the algorithm concludes while at round
r^{\prime\prime}
. Now let
q
the probability that the algorithm terminates before round
r^{\prime\prime}
. We then have

\displaystyle\E[f_{R}]

\displaystyle\leq q\cdot f_{r^{\prime\prime}-1}+(1-q)\sum_{r\geq r^{\prime\prime}}f_{r}\cdot\frac{5}{9}\cdot\left\lparen\frac{4}{9}\right\rparen^{r-r^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq f_{r^{\prime\prime}}+f_{r^{\prime\prime}}\sum_{i\geq 0}2^{i}\left\lparen\frac{4}{9}\right\rparen^{i}=O(f_{r^{\prime\prime}})

as claimed.
∎

Lemma C.4

(Bound on Expected Cost)
.

The cost of the clustering in Algorithm
8
is at most
O(W^{*})
in expectation.

Proof.

The proof is nearly identical to Theorem 2 of
[
LSS16
]
.

Fix a cluster
S_{i}^{*}
and a ring
\tau
. We first consider the cost of points in
S_{i,\tau}^{*}
before the first point from
S_{i,\tau}^{*}
was chosen as a center. Note that each
j\in S_{i,\tau}^{*}
is chosen with probability at least
p_{j}\geq\min\{d(\mathbf{a}_{j},C)^{p}/f_{R},1\}
, where
C
is the set of centers chosen by time
j
. Then by Lemma
C.2
, the sum of these costs is bounded by
f_{R}
, and summing over all
i
and
\tau
gives a bound of
O(f_{R}k\log n)
. By Lemma
C.3
, this is
O(W^{*})
.

We next consider the cost of points in
S_{i,\tau}^{*}
after the first point from
S_{i,\tau}^{*}
was chosen as a center. Then as shown in the proof of Lemma
C.1
, the cost of these points is at most
O(W^{*})
.
∎
